# Decisão da Comissão, de 10/06/2025 relativa à compatibilidade com o mercado comum de uma operação de concentração (Processo no COMP/M.11602 - SES / INTELSAT) com base no Regulamento (CE) n. 139/2004 do Conselho (Apenas faz fé o texto em língua inglesa)

Subject:    Case M.11602 – SES / INTELSAT
            Commission decision pursuant to Article 6(1)(b) of Council Regulation
            No 139/2004 (1) and Article 57 of the Agreement on the European
            Economic Area (2)



Dear Sir or Madam,


(1)     On 29 April 2025, the European Commission (‘Commission’) received
        notification of a proposed concentration pursuant to Article 4 following a referral
        pursuant to Article 4(5) of the Merger Regulation by which SES S.A. (‘SES’ or
        ‘Notifying Party’, Luxembourg) intends to acquire sole control of Intelsat
        Holdings S.à.r.l. (‘Intelsat’, Luxembourg) (the ‘Transaction’). (3) SES and
        Intelsat are collectively referred to as the ‘Parties’.




( 1)   OJ L 24, 29.1.2004, p. 1 (the ‘Merger Regulation’). With effect from 1 December 2009, the Treaty
       on the Functioning of the European Union (‘TFEU’) has introduced certain changes, such as the
       replacement of ‘Community’ by ‘Union’ and ‘common market’ by ‘internal market’. The
       terminology of the TFEU will be used throughout this decision.
( 2)   OJ L 1, 3.1.1994, p. 3 (the ‘EEA Agreement’).
( 3)   OJ C, C/2025/2725, 8.5.2025.
1.      THE PARTIES
(2)     SES is a publicly listed company headquartered in Luxembourg, which owns and
        operates a fleet of 43 geostationary Earth orbit (‘GEO’) and 26 medium Earth
        orbit (‘MEO’) satellites. In addition, SES offers satellite services for a range of
        use cases.
(3)     Intelsat, headquartered in Luxembourg and with administrative headquarters in
        the United States, owns and operates a fleet of 54 GEO satellites. Intelsat also
        provides satellite services for various use cases.


2.      THE CONCENTRATION
(4)     The Transaction consists in the acquisition of sole control by SES over Intelsat
        (currently controlled by Allianz SE (Germany)), pursuant to a share purchase
        agreement dated 30 April 2024, according to which SES will acquire all the
        outstanding shares in Intelsat for a total consideration of c. EUR 2.8 billion, in
        addition to certain contingent value rights. (4) Upon closing of the Transaction,
        Intelsat will be wholly owned by SES.
(5)     Therefore, the Transaction constitutes a concentration within the meaning of
        Article 3(1)(b) of the Merger Regulation.


3.      UNION DIMENSION
(6)     The Transaction does not have a Union dimension within the meaning of Article
        1(2) of the Merger Regulation, because the combined aggregate worldwide
        turnover of the undertakings concerned does not exceed EUR 5 000 million, and
        Intelsat’s Union-wide turnover does not exceed EUR 250 million. Nor does the
        Transaction have a Union dimension according to Article 1(3) of the Merger
        Regulation, because the combined aggregate turnover of the undertakings
        concerned does not exceed EUR 100 million in each of at least three Member
        States.
(7)     Nonetheless, the Transaction fulfils the two conditions for a referral request set
        out in Article 4(5) of the Merger Regulation since it is a concentration within the
        meaning of Article 3 of the Merger Regulation, and it is capable of being reviewed
        under the national competition laws of at least three Member States. In this case,
        Cyprus, France and Germany were capable of reviewing the Transaction as it
        meets their national turnover-based thresholds.
(8)     On 18 October 2024, the Notifying Party, by means of a reasoned submission,
        requested that the Transaction be examined by the Commission pursuant to
        Article 4(5) of the Merger Regulation. A copy of that submission was transmitted
        to the Member States on 18 October 2024. The Member States competent to
        examine the Transaction did not express disagreement to the referral request
        within the period laid down by the Merger Regulation.




( 4)   Contingent value rights holders will receive from SES a contingent cash distribution for future
       spectrum clearing (which may be imposed by the United States Federal Communications
       Commission) on a pro rata basis. The amount and timing of any such distribution is contingent upon
       the occurrence of certain events within seven and one-half years after the closing of the Transaction.
       Form CO, paras 225-28

(9)     Accordingly, the Transaction is deemed to have a Union-dimension pursuant to
        Article 4(5) of the Merger Regulation.


4.      INDUSTRY OVERVIEW
(10)    The industry sector on which the Commission has assessed the impact of the
        Transaction on competition is the satellite communications sector.
(11)    Satellites retransmit information/signals from one point to one or more points,
        thus enabling customers’ data to be moved between different points. Both Parties
        own and operate a diverse fleet of satellites. (5)
(12)    In summary, satellite communications have three main parts: (6)
        (a)     Uplink: a signal that is sent to a specific satellite from a user terminal or
                from a satellite teleport or gateway Earth station.
        (b)     Transponder: a satellite component that, once it receives the signal,
                amplifies the signal’s strength and alter its frequency before sending it back
                to a designated Earth station(s); and
        (c)     Downlink: these are the signals transmitted back to Earth by the satellite to
                user terminals or satellite teleport or gateway Earth stations.

4.1.    The satellite communications supply chain
(13)    The supply of satellite communication services involves a variety of players
        operating at different levels of the supply chain. The satellite communications
        supply chain comprises the following main levels: (7)
        (a)     Satellite Network Operators (‘SNOs’): companies that own and manage
                their own satellite fleets. (8) SNOs supply satellite capacity for satellite
                communications (‘satellite capacity’) at the wholesale level (9) to Satellite
                Service Providers (‘SSPs’) and/or resellers (for resale to downstream
                customers), and/or they use their capacity captively to sell satellite
                connectivity services directly to end customers (i.e. by acting as a SSP). (10)
        (b)     SSPs: companies that assemble packages of satellite connectivity solutions
                consisting of satellite capacity (either purchased from third-party SNOs or
                sourced internally in the case of vertically integrated SNOs/SSPs) and
                related services such as the satellite operator’s own Earth stations,
                equipment (e.g. terminals), or associated services (e.g. invoicing tools,
                customer support, remote activation of handsets and traffic monitoring)
                (‘satellite services’), which they sell to resellers or end-customers.
        (c)     Value added resellers (‘VARs’ or ‘resellers’): companies that purchase
                satellite capacity and/or services from SSPs (including from vertically

( 5)   The size and mass of each satellite range from smaller, lighter models to large, high-capacity
       satellites exceeding 6 000 kg in mass, and are tailored to the specific mission requirements and the
       technological capabilities available at the time of its construction.
( 6)   See https://www.intelsat.com/resources/tools/satellite-101/, visited on 6 June 2025.
( 7)   Commission decision of 25 May 2023 in case M.10807 - Viasat/Inmarsat, recital 18.
( 8)   The leading SNOs have fleets that typically cover most of the world. Regional SNOs have fleets
       typically covering one or more continents and may rely in part on capacity leased from other SNOs
       to supply services to different parts of the globe.
( 9)   SNOs are therefore active at the upstream level of the economic chain.
(10)   The extent to which SNOs are vertically integrated, i.e., operate at both the wholesale and the retail
       level, varies between SNOs.

                integrated SSPs) and distribute them to end-customers. In some instances,
                VARs operate as a distribution channel for SSPs. In other instances, VARs
                provide additional value-added services to end-customers (e.g. installation
                and maintenance of equipment) and even compete against SSPs.

4.2.    The different satellite orbits
(14)    When rockets launch satellites, they put them into orbit in space. There, gravity
        keeps the satellite on its required orbit. Satellite connectivity can be served from
        satellites orbiting in different orbits.
(15)    GEO satellites circle Earth above the equator from west to east following Earth’s
        rotation by travelling at exactly the same rate as Earth. This makes satellites in
        GEO appear to be ‘stationary’ over a fixed position on Earth (‘orbital
        location’). (11)
(16)    Over time, non-geostationary orbit (‘NGSO’) satellites were launched. Those
        occupy a range of orbital locations and do not maintain a stationary position, but
        instead move in relation to the Earth’s surface. NGSO satellites include:
        (a)     The most recently developed NGSOs are low Earth orbit (‘LEO’) satellites.
                (12) On the emergence of LEO operators, see Section 4.6.1;
        (b)     MEO satellites; (13) and
        (c)     Highly elliptical orbit satellites. (14)

4.3.    The different types of satellites
(17)    Satellites can be further divided into:
        (a)     Traditional (‘wide-beam’) satellites, which deploy a single beam across a
                wide geographic area; and
        (b)     High-throughput satellites (‘HTS’), which deploy multiple narrow spot-
                beams (instead of a single wide-beam) that re-use the frequency, so that a
                single satellite can deliver a multiple of the data throughput delivered by
                wide-beam satellites.
(18)    Historically, satellites were GEO wide-beam satellites. However, satellite
        operators are increasingly shifting away from wide-beam satellites towards HTS
        because HTS deliver significantly increased capacity when compared to
        traditional wide-beam satellites. (15)




(11)   GEO satellites are positioned in an orbit of c. 36 000 kilometres above the Earth’s surface.
(12)   LEO satellites are positioned c. 500 – 2 000 kilometres above the ground. Since LEOs orbit closer
       to the Earth’s surface than GEOs, the latency of LEOs is lower. Furthermore, since the satellite
       footprint decreases in size as the orbit becomes lower, a greater number of LEO (compared to GEO)
       satellites is required to provide continuous global services: to give constant coverage LEOs often
       work as part of a large combination or constellation of multiple satellites that sometimes are
       launched together to create a ‘net’ around the Earth.
(13)   MEO satellites are positioned c. 2 000 – 36 000 kilometres above the Earth’s surface.
(14)   Highly elliptical orbit satellites operate in orbits where the satellite’s path around the Earth is shaped
       like an elongated ellipse rather than a circle, making them ideal for continuous coverage of polar or
       near-polar regions.
(15)   Commission decision of 25 May 2023 in case M.10807 - Viasat/Inmarsat, recital 36; Form CO,
       paras 83-85.
                                                      4
4.4.    The different types of frequencies
(19)    Satellite capacity is, depending on the frequency band, typically measured in
        Megahertz (‘MHz’) or Gigahertz (‘GHz’), which is a measure of frequency i.e.,
        how many cycles or events occur per second. (16) Satellite communications
        services use different frequencies in the satellite spectrum (17) to exchange
        signals.
(20)    Frequency bands are separated into two categories:
        (a)     Lower frequency bands, e.g. L-band (1-2 GHz’) or S-band (2-4 GHz),
                which have less bandwidth and are, therefore, less suitable for data-
                intensive applications but are viewed as more reliable and, thus, more
                suitable for critical applications (‘narrowband’).
        (b)     Higher frequency bands, e.g. Ku-band (12-18 GHz) or Ka-band (26-40
                GHz), which have more bandwidth and, therefore, offer greater
                communications capacity but are more susceptible to signal interference,
                including degradation owing to ‘rain fade’ (‘broadband’). The Parties also
                consider the C-band (4.0-8.0 GHz) and the X-band (8.0-12.0 GHz) to be
                broadband frequencies, as they share similar qualities with regards to
                bandwidth and throughput. (18)
(21)    While there is an EU regulatory framework, broadcasting is not technically
        harmonized at the EU level. (19) The use of frequency bands is allocated and
        decided worldwide at the International Telecommunication Union (‘ITU’ (20))’s
        World Radio Conference (‘WRC’), which takes place every four or five years.
        (21) The ITU WRC allocation does not concern how much (volume) capacity is
        used by satellite operators, but rather whether a particular communication service
        can be used in a specific radio frequency band and under which conditions so that



(16)   1 MHz = 1 million cycles per second; 1 GHz = 1 billion cycles per second.
(17)   Terrestrial spectrum refers to the radio frequencies assigned for ground-based transmission systems:
       Signals are transmitted from towers or antennas on the Earth’s surface and the coverage is
       geographically limited, depending on terrain, transmitter power, and antenna placement. On the
       other hand, satellite spectrum refers to radio frequencies assigned to satellite communication i.e.,
       transmissions between Earth stations and orbiting satellites, and frequencies can be higher than
       terrestrial.

(19)   Directive 2018/1972 of 11 December 2018 establishing the European Electronic Communications
       Code, OJ L 321 17.12.2018, p. 36, defines ‘harmonised radio spectrum’ ‘for which harmonised
       conditions relating to its availability and efficient use have been established by way of technical
       implementing measures in accordance with Article 4 of Decision No 676/2002/EC [on a regulatory
       framework for radio spectrum policy in the EU (Radio Spectrum Decision)]’, and there is a special
       procedure described in such Radio Spectrum Decision for the Commission to adopt a so called
       implementing decision. No such implementing decision exists in the case of spectrum used for
       broadcasting, whether satellite or terrestrial (for the latter, there is a so-called UHF decision, which
       preserves the sub-700 MHz band for the terrestrial broadcasting).
(20)   The ITU is the United Nations specialised agency for telecommunications.
(21)   While the ITU sets the global framework, national regulators (like the FCC in the U.S. or BNetzA
       (Federal Network Agency) in Germany) decide how to implement these allocations within their
       own countries. At the WRC, a predefined subset of ITU Radio Regulations (‘RRs’) is reviewed
       and, if deemed necessary, revised. The ITU RRs are an international treaty between ITU Member
       States, which are binding under international law.

4.5.    The different end uses of satellite communications
(22)    Satellite communications are used for end uses in different industry sectors. Those
        end uses or industry sectors primarily include:
        (a)    Broadcasting (i.e., the distribution of audio or video content from a single
               source to many recipients) like satellite television or satellite radio (‘media
               purposes’ or ‘media’);
        (b)    Data communications between fixed points/terminals/antennas (e.g.,
               satellite mobile backhaul, trunking, internet access and enterprise
               connectivity) (‘fixed data’); (23)
        (c)    Connectivity for government customers for both military and commercial
               applications (‘government’);
        (d)    Connectivity for maritime customers (‘maritime’); and
        (e)    Connectivity for aviation customers (‘aviation’).
(23)    Satellite communications used for media purposes are called one-way satellite
        communications. Satellite capacity that uses radio frequency spectrum that is
        allocated for the Broadcasting Satellite Services (‘BSS’), is used to distribute the
        same signal (e.g., television or radio content) over a large area, whether
        (a)    For direct-to-home (‘DTH’) reception, which can also be free-to-air
               (‘FTA’) when the satellite capacity is provided to ad-supported content
               providers that want their content to reach a large population of viewers free
               of charge with inexpensive dishes pointed at the satellite operator’s satellite;
               or
        (b)    Reception at a terrestrial broadcast station or cable system for re-
               transmission (‘cable’).
(24)    Conversely, two-way satellite communications allow end-to-end exchanges of
        voice and data among two or more terminals. Satellite capacity that uses radio
        frequency spectrum that is allocated for the Fixed Satellite Services (‘FSS’), is
        the most common mode of communication for fixed data, government, maritime
        and aviation connectivity. (24)

4.6.    Competitive dynamics and trends
(25)    The satellite sector is undergoing a period of change characterised by the
        emergence of new players, as well as the emergence of alternative technologies
        to satellite connectivity.

       a ‘fixed’ point on the Earth.

4.6.1.    Emergence of LEO operators
(26)      In recent years, several new entrant LEO satellite operators have launched or are
          expected to continue to launch large-scale LEO constellations comprised of HTS
          satellites. Such players include: (25)
          (a)    SpaceX’s Starlink, which is reported to have c. 7 000 operational satellites
                 at the time of this Decision and plans to launch c. 42 000 additional
                 satellites; (26)
          (b)    Eutelsat’s OneWeb, which was formed through the combination of Eutelsat
                 and OneWeb in 2023, becoming an integrated GEO-LEO satellite operator
                 with a fleet of 35 GEO satellites and a LEO constellation of more than 600
                 satellites; (27)
          (c)    Telesat’s Lightspeed, which are c. 200 LEO satellites; (28)
          (d)    Amazon’s Project Kuiper, with plans to launch c. 3 200 satellites; (29)
          (e)    Others, including Chinese constellations.
(27)      According to the Parties, demand for satellite capacity is projected to grow
          significantly both globally and in Europe within the next couple of years. Within
          the period 2022-32, the Parties expect, based on third party reports, an average
          annual growth rate of 33% globally and of 28.6% in Europe. (30) On a global level,
          the Parties submit that demand will grow from 2 700 Gbps in 2022 by more than
          17-times to 46 700 Gbps in 2032. (31)
(28)      Correspondingly, in particular the entry of LEO HTS constellations has led to an
          increase in available satellite capacity. The Parties estimate, based on an analysis
          of data in a third-party report (32), that global capacity supply is projected to grow
          more than 13 times from 44 488 Gbps in 2022 to 586 287 Gbps in 2032. Most of
          this growth is expected to come from NGSO constellations, which already
          provided 91.8% of the satellite capacity in 2022 and are projected to constitute
          around 95% of global satellite capacity supply from 2028 onwards. GEO wide-
          beam satellites are estimated to decrease, whereas GEO HTS satellite capacity
          will increase. Overall, based on the data submitted by the Parties, GEO satellites
          provided a total capacity of 3 650 Gbps in 2022 and are expected to grow by about
          8 times in capacity until 2032 to 29 300 Gbps. (33)

4.6.2.    Modern terrestrial alternatives to satellite media distribution
(30)      Terrestrial and satellite broadcasting coexist. (35) Media companies have a range
          of distribution methods to choose from, ranging from traditional broadcast, cable,
          and satellite distribution to Internet-based streaming and on-demand services. (36)
          Beyond traditional broadcast towers or cable TV networks, terrestrial alternatives
          to satellite distribution today also include Internet Protocol (‘IP’)-based terrestrial
          distribution, (37) as reflected below in Figure 1:
          (a)     OTT (over-the-top) distribution method i.e., the delivery of media content
                  over the Internet, bypassing traditional cable, broadcast, and satellite TV
                  platforms; (38) and/or
          (b)     Fibre technology i.e., a type of internet infrastructure that uses fibre-optic
                  cables to deliver very high-speed internet.

          examples include Netflix, YouTube, Hulu, Disney+, Amazon Prime Video. Such
          OTT streaming services can be accessed using any broadband Internet
          connection, which, in turn, can be supplied by either

(35)     Terrestrial broadcasting (using radio towers and antennas) predates satellite broadcasting, which
         was introduced to extend coverage to areas where terrestrial networks could not reach effectively
         (remote or rural areas, large countries, oceans). With the rollout of digital terrestrial networks (i.e.
         the evolution of traditional or analogue signals), terrestrial became an alternative to satellite,
         especially in urban and suburban areas, thanks to lower cost and ease of access.

         streaming, local networks, IP-based television, and more.
(38)     The infrastructure that accelerates and scales OTT delivery is content delivery network (‘CDN’),
         as mentioned in Figure 1.

5.2.1.    The Parties’ activities
(37)      Both Parties are vertically integrated SNO/SSP players (paragraph (13)) (47)
(38)      Both Parties supply satellite capacity for one-way satellite communications and
          two-way satellite communications (paragraphs (23) and (24)). (48)

          (paragraph (22)), even though the Parties are not always at the same levels of the

          (a)    For media, both Parties offer to broadcasters and Pay-TV operators (i.e., not
                 the viewers) DTH-pay distribution, FTA distribution and cable services
                 (paragraph (23)) (50), mainly from wide-beam satellites (only Intelsat uses
                 an HTS satellite) (paragraph (17)). (51) The Parties do not share the same
                 geographic focus: Intelsat is less present in Europe and the scope of the
                 customers served by SES is broader than the scope of customers served by
                 Intelsat;
          (b)    For fixed data, both Parties provide satellite capacity and satellite services
                 to SSPs and end-customers (e.g., mobile network operators); (52)
          (c)    For government, both Parties provide satellite capacity and satellite services
                 to SSPs and end-customers;
          (d)    For maritime, both Parties provide satellite capacity to SSPs; in addition,
                 SES (but not Intelsat) contracts directly with end customers for the

          (e)    For aviation, both Parties provide satellite capacity to SSPs; in addition,
                 Intelsat (but not SES) contracts directly with end customers for the
                 provision of satellite services. (54)

5.2.2.    Product market definition

5.2.2.1. Commission’s precedents
(45)      First, the Commission in its recent decision Viasat/Inmarsat considered that the
          market for satellite communication services could be segmented based on the
          level of the supply chain, i.e., distinguishing between SNOs, SSPs and VARs, and
          that therefore there exists a separate market for the supply of satellite capacity by
          SNOs. (65)
(46)      Second, the Commission in its previous decisions found that one-way and two-
          way satellite communications are not part of the same product market. (66)
(47)      Third, the Commission considered, but ultimately left open, whether the market
          for ‘satellite transponder capacity’ could be further segmented between satellite

        capacity used for broadcasting (or the distribution of TV signals) and other
        telecommunication services (such as telephony or data services). (67)
(48)    Fourth, the Commission indicated that the market for the supply of satellite
        capacity should likely be segmented between broadband and narrowband satellite
        capacity because ‘a majority of market participants (both GEO and LEO SNOs)
        considering that those products were not substitutable because of different
        product characteristics and intended use on the demand-side’, but ultimately left
        the market definition open. (68)
(49)    Fifth, the Commission considered the segmentation between GEO and
        NGSO/LEO satellite capacity but left the market definition open because, while
        ‘a majority of market participants considered the market should not be segmented
        between GEO and NGSO/LEO satellite capacity’, ‘most market participants
        noted that there are some product characteristic differences between GEO and
        NGSO/LEO satellite capacity (e.g. latency, reliability, and price)’. (69)
(50)    Sixth, the Commission considered the segmentation between HTS and non-HTS
        (wide-beam) satellite capacity but left the market definition open because ‘the
        results of the market investigation were mixed […] Market participants indicated
        that these potential segmentations were substitutable in some instances and not
        substitutable in other instances’. (70)
(51)    Seventh, the Commission considered the segmentation based on individual
        frequency bands, i.e., between Ka-band and Ku-band, but left the market
        definition open because ‘the market investigation also provided mixed results […]
        The majority of market participants indicated that in some instances Ka-band and
        Ku-band satellite capacity are substitutable, but not in other instances’. (71)
(52)    Eighth, the Commission considered, but ultimately left open, whether the market
        for satellite transponder capacity could be further segmented between different
        orbital locations because ‘while the majority of respondents confirmed that
        switching is a difficult, lengthy and costly process for TV distributors, which
        requires the repointing of customers’ satellite dishes, they also provided several
        past switching examples between satellite operators in Europe’. (72)
(53)    Ninth, the Commission has not considered in its previous decisions a possible
        segmentation of the market for the supply of satellite capacity for media purposes
        by form of distribution, namely DTH, FTA or cable.

5.2.2.2. Notifying Party’s views
(54)    First, the Notifying Party submits that for assessing the Transaction the
        appropriate relevant product market is the overall market for the supply of satellite
        capacity with no sub-segmentation by industry segment, one- and two-way
        communications, orbit type, satellite type, or frequency band. (73)
(55)    Second, the Notifying Party submits that the supply of satellite capacity should
        not be sub-segmented by:
        (a)    Industry segment (e.g., aviation, government etc.) because satellite capacity
               is fungible across the different industry segments. According to the
               Notifying Party, satellite capacity is a commodity product that is used by
               end customers or SSPs for different applications across all industry
               segments, and customers are not treated differently across the various
               industries; (74) or
        (b)    One-way or two-way satellite communications, because: (i) satellite
               capacity is fungible and any ‘one-way’ or ‘two-way’ characterization
               relates to its usage; (75) (ii) the two types of spectrum, FSS and BSS, are
               interchangeable; (76) (iii) most satellites can be used interchangeably for
               two-way and one-way satellite communications. (77)
(56)    According to the Notifying Party, segmenting the market into one-way and two-
        way satellite communications is broadly analogous to segmenting the market into
        satellite capacity supplied in the media segment and satellite capacity supplied to
        non-media customers. As such, the same arguments set out above against the
        segmentation of satellite capacity by industry segment (paragraph (55)(a)) apply
        equally to the segmentation of satellite capacity by one-way or two-way satellite
        communications (paragraph (55)(b)).
(57)    Third, as regards specifically the media industry (described in paragraph (22)(a)),
        the Notifying Party acknowledges that it has unique features such as ‘the ability
        to identify customers in the Media segment based on their requirements’, ‘wide-
        beam satellites may be well suited for media distribution applications’ and ‘an
        additional horizontal / spatial differentiation factor […]: orbital location and the
        number of consumer antennas pointed at that orbital location’. (78)
(58)    Fourth, the Notifying Party does not consider it necessary to provide a definitive
        view in relation to potential segmentation in respect of narrowband satellite
        capacity versus broadband satellite capacity, given that the Parties do not supply

        satellite capacity that the Commission considers to be ‘narrowband’ (paragraph
        (41)) and there is therefore no overlap in this regard. (79)
(59)    Fifth, the Notifying Party submits that it is not appropriate to distinguish between
        GEO versus NGSO/LEO satellite capacity because: (i) the Parties compete with
        LEO satellite operators at each level of the supply chain and each of the industry
        segments; (80) and (ii) NGSO satellite capacity is largely replacing GEO satellite
        capacity as there has been substitution by customers away from GEO satellites to
        NGSO (and in particular, LEO) satellites. (81) In addition, the satellite
        communications industry is in the process of shifting towards a ‘frequency- and
        hardware-agnostic environment’, and customers are increasingly looking towards
        multi-band and multi-orbit solutions. (82)
(60)    Sixth, the Notifying Party submits that HTS and wide-beam satellite capacity are
        substitutable, (83) because: (i) SNOs are increasingly shifting away from wide-
        beam satellites towards HTS, as HTS satellites cost less and have a much higher
        volume of capacity, making them more attractive for most industry segments; (84)
        and (ii) while wide-beam satellites remain a customer preference for media
        purposes, HTS satellites can also be used in the media sector, which is constrained
        by IP-based terrestrial distribution described in paragraph (30). (85)
(61)    Seventh, the Notifying Party submits that a sub-segmentation of the supply of
        satellite capacity by frequency band is not warranted because: (i) there is a high
        degree of demand side-substitutability across the individual frequency bands.
        Customers often procure satellite capacity across all frequency bands. Also,
        multi-band solutions are being increasingly employed with multiple vendors
        procuring tri-band antennas capable of operating in the C-, Ka- and Ku-bands; (86)
        (ii) in the media segment, both the C-band (most commonly used outside of
        Europe for the delivery of cable TV programming to cable headends) and the Ku-
        band (most commonly used in Europe for DTH satellite television services to
        customers such as Sky or Canal+) (87) are used for media distribution; and (iii)
        there are very limited exceptions to substitutability such as the most common use
        of Ku-band in Europe or the use of certain bans for military applications. (88)

5.2.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(64)    In line with its previous practice (paragraph (45)) as well as the Notifying Party’s
        views (paragraph (54)), the Commission took as a starting point for its assessment
        a separate market for the supply of satellite capacity. The market investigation has
        not revealed any reasons to deviate from its precedents in which it defined a
        distinct market for the supply of satellite capacity. (91)
           i.    Segmentation between one-way and two-way satellite capacity
(65)    Segmenting the market into one-way and two-way satellite communications is
        broadly analogous to segmenting the market into satellite capacity supplied for
        media purposes and satellite capacity supplied for other industries (as the
        Notifying Party acknowledges, as noted in paragraph (56)).
(66)    Against this background, the Commission in the market investigation tested
        whether the market for the supply of satellite capacity should be segmented
        between one-way satellite communications for media purposes and two-way
        satellite communications used in other industries, namely aviation, maritime,
        fixed data and government.
(67)    The vast majority of respondents having expressed a view submitted that the
        distinction between one-way and two-way satellite capacity is still relevant
        today. (92)
(68)    From a demand-side perspective, for example, one customer submitted that ‘one-
        way satellite capacity is specifically designed for media broadcasting with
        different technical requirements, pricing structures, and usage patterns compared
        to two-way capacity, which is intended for interactive services like internet
        access’. Another respondent submitted that ‘one-way traffic uses different codecs,
        protocols, methods and serves different purposes compared to two-way traffic.
        One-way traffic is about one-to-many broadcasting with high capacity and high
        reliability streams. As such, there are other pricing and business models, other
        service level agreements and different reliability standards have to be met’.
(69)    From a supply-side perspective, for example, one respondent submitted that ‘one-
        way satellite capacity supports broadcast-like, point-to-multipoint distribution
        (e.g., linear traditional broadcast TV, radio), where interaction or uplink

       only has one shared orbital position (with Eutelsat at 28.2°/28.5° East) and Intelsat also only has
       one shared orbital position at a different location (with Space Norway at 1° West).
(90)   Form CO, para. 452.
(91)   Replies to Q1 - Questionnaire on one-way satellite capacity (media) to customers and competitors
       (‘Q1 (Media)’), and Q2 - Questionnaire on two-way satellite capacity to customers and competitors
       (‘Q2 (Two-way capacity)’).
(92)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.1; and Q2 (Two-way capacity), question C.A.1.

        capability is unnecessary. In contrast, two-way satellite capacity enables
        interactive, bidirectional communication essential for broadband internet, cloud
        applications, and enterprise connectivity’. One competitor submitted that, while
        ‘SNO can easily make available [satellite capacity] for different customers or
        services’, ‘the big impact of this change is related to the user equipment, terminals
        and field services since the use of this capacity will suppose to buy, deploy an
        [sic] install a [sic] new terminals. One-way and two-way services use different
        ground segment infrastructure and user terminals’. (93)
(70)    In addition, the market investigation confirmed that the supply of one-way
        satellite capacity for media purposes is a separate market from the supply of two-
        way satellite capacity for other industries, such as aviation, maritime,
        government, and fixed data.
(71)    From a demand-side perspective, the Commission’s market investigation tested
        whether the one-way satellite capacity for media purposes is substitutable or
        interchangeable with the two-way satellite capacity for other industries/end-uses
        (for example, aviation, maritime, government, fixed data), based on their product
        characteristics, prices and intended use. The majority of respondents having
        expressed a view excluded such substitutability. (94)
(72)    For example, one customer submitted that ‘the applications and objectives are
        different[:] on the one hand, there are the efficient and mass-capable DTH
        distribution of high-quality linear TV services and media content (one-way)
        [and], on the other hand, the individual commercial data and Internet services
        based on unicast connections (two-way)’. One competitor submitted that ‘this
        distinction is visible across several dimensions: - Satellite technology:
        geostationary wide-beam satellites for one-way vs geostationary or non-
        geostationary high-throughput (mostly) satellites for two-way - Customers of
        satellite capacity: media broadcasters for one-way (mostly) vs satellite services
        providers, telecommunication operators, government/defence agencies and
        broadband Internet end-users for two-way - User terminals: traditional one-way
        parabolic antennas for one-way vs more complex and more expensive terminals
        able to transmit and receive for two-way’.
(73)    From a supply-side perspective, the Commission’s market investigation tested
        whether SNOs can switch easily and quickly between the supply of two-way
        satellite capacity for other industries/end-uses (for example, aviation, maritime,
        government, fixed data) to the supply of one-way satellite capacity for media
        purposes. The majority of respondents having expressed a view excluded such
        switching possibility. (95)
(74)    For example, one competitor submitted that ‘the big impact of this change is
        related to the user equipment, terminals and field services since the use of this
        capacity will supposed to buy, deploy an [sic] install a [sic]new terminals. One-
        way and two-way services use different ground segment infrastructure and user
        terminals. Moreover, typical GEO one-way satellite capacity is wide-beam which
        is less efficient for two-ways broadband applications compared to new HTS and
        LEO services which make GEO wide-beam capacity less competitive in terms of
        pricing for these two-way applications’; another competitor submitted that ‘two
        instances of one-way communications cannot replace one instsance [sic] of a two-

        way communications system in a practical, commercially viable manner due to
        spectrum limitations, up-link lisences [sic], equipment compitability and
        equipment availability and transponder characterisitcs [sic](EIRP, G/T,
        bandwidth, etc)’; another competitor submitted that its ‘internal organization
        reflects this distinction, with two separate business units, one dedicated to video
        applications (one-way) and the other to connectivity applications (two-way)’.
        Another respondent submitted that ‘switching between these services involves
        significant technical, operational, and regulatory barriers, confirming that they
        remain separate and non-substitutable markets’.
(75)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that separate relevant product
        markets exist for the supply of one-way satellite capacity used for media purposes,
        on the one hand, and the supply two-way satellite capacity used in other non-
        media industries (such as aviation, maritime, government and fixed data), on the
        other hand.
          ii.    Segmentation between broadband and narrowband satellite capacity
(76)    The market investigation confirmed that broadband and narrowband (96) one-way
        satellite capacity used for media purposes, as well as broadband and narrowband
        two-way satellite capacity used in other non-media industries, do not belong to
        the same market.
(77)    From a demand-side perspective, the vast majority of respondents excluded that
        broadband satellite capacity is substitutable with narrowband satellite capacity,
        based on product characteristics, prices and intended uses. (97)
(78)    From a supply-side perspective, the majority of SNOs that expressed a view
        excluded that SNOs could switch easily from supplying broadband satellite
        capacity to supplying narrowband satellite capacity in short time and without
        incurring significant costs. (98)
(79)    In any event, for the purposes of this Decision the market definition can remain
        open as concerns the segmentation between broadband and narrowband, as
        neither of the Parties supply narrowband capacity (paragraph (41)).
(80)    In this Decision, the Commission will conduct its assessment on the basis of the
        narrowest plausible market relevant for the Transaction encompassing only
        broadband satellite capacity, because neither of the Parties offers narrowband
        capacity. (99)
         iii.    Conclusion
(81)    In light of the market feedback set out above, in the remainder of this Decision
        the Commission carries out its assessment separately for: (i) the supply of
        broadband one-way satellite capacity for media purposes (‘one-way satellite

         capacity (for media purposes)’), and (ii) the supply of broadband two-way
         satellite capacity used in other non-media industries, such as aviation, maritime,

5.2.2.3.1.      Market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes)
(82)     In the market of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes), the market
         investigation has tested whether there are separate segments between: (i) orbit
         types such as GEO or LEO satellites; (ii) satellite type such as traditional/wide-
         beam technology or HTS/newer technology; (iii) frequency bands; (iv) orbital
         locations; and (v) distribution types such as DTH, FTA or cable. (100)

5.2.2.3.1.1.    Segmentation between GEO and LEO one-way satellite capacity (for media
                purposes)
(83)     The market investigation’s results indicate that one-way satellite capacity (for
         media purposes) from GEO satellites and capacity from LEO satellites (such as
         Starlink or Eutelsat’s OneWeb) do not belong to the same relevant product
         market.
(84)     From a demand-side perspective, the vast majority of respondents having
         expressed a view excluded that the one-way satellite capacity provided by LEO
         satellites is substitutable with the one-way satellite capacity provided by GEO
         satellites. (101) For example, one customer submitted that ‘DTH over one-way
         capacity based on GEO satellite is optimized and very efficient for the reception
         of broadband linear TV services via fixed satellite dishes with high gain. Tracked
         or omnidirectional LEO receiving antennas have a much lower antenna gain and
         are therefore better suited for other applications’. Another customer submitted
         ‘LEO use completely different technology and customers end equipment’. (102)
(85)     From a supply-side perspective, the vast majority of SNOs having expressed a
         view excluded that SNOs could switch easily and quickly between the supply of
         LEO-satellite-based capacity and the supply of GEO-satellite-based capacity.
         (103) For example, a competitor submitted that ‘[w]hen developing, deploying and
         operationalizing a LEO satellite fleet, an SNO would incur significant costs (both
         CAPEX and OPEX) and risks. […] The operation of a LEO constellation also
         presents significant differences with the operation of a GEO asset (e.g., ability to
         control hundreds of assets around the globe, consistent ground network able to
         feed the satellites, etc.). Marketing LEO capacity instead of GEO capacity is
         challenging, as most (if not all) user terminals compatible with GEO capacity for
         media purposes are, currently, not compatible with LEO capacity and country-
         per-country regulations to sell satellite capacity and/or services generally differs
         between GEO and LEO, forcing the SNO to get additional licenses for LEO
         capacity, in a costly and timely effort’. (104)
(86)     The exact market definition can be left open for the purposes of this Decision, as
         the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the

        in the negative. Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.13.

         internal market irrespective of whether a narrower market comprising solely GEO
         capacity, or a broader market comprising both GEO and LEO capacity, is defined.
         In this Decision, the Commission will consider the market of the one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes) from GEO satellites as the narrowest plausible
         market relevant for the Transaction, because the Parties do not own or operate any
         LEO satellites (paragraph (42)) and LEOs are not considered an alternative for
         one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) (as explained in paragraph (326).

5.2.2.3.1.2.    Segmentation between HTS and wide-beam one-way satellite capacity (for
                media purposes)
(87)     The market investigation’s results are inconclusive on whether one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes) from HTS (newer technology) satellites and
         capacity from wide-beam (traditional technology) satellites belong to the same
         relevant product market.
(88)     The majority of respondents having expressed a view excluded that one-way
         capacity provided with HTS is substitutable with wide-beam satellite capacity.
         (105) From a demand-side perspective, several respondents referred to different
         technical and commercial functions. For example, one customer submitted that
         ‘HTS capacity is not substitutable or interchangeable with wide beam satellite
         capacity in the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity for media
         purposes. These two capacity types serve fundamentally different technical and
         commercial functions’. (106)
(89)     From a supply side substitutability, several respondents referred to the
         cost/investment required to offer HTS technology as compared to wide-beam
         technology. For example, one respondent submitted that ‘the adoption of HTS for
         OB [outside broadcasting] often requires different and potentially more complex
         ground equipment, including antennas capable of tracking or utilizing multiple
         spot beams, and modems optimized for the higher frequencies and modulation
         schemes employed by HTS. This infrastructure investment and the intricacies of
         managing connectivity across multiple beams present significant hurdles to a
         direct and seamless substitution for established GEO-based OB setups, which
         typically rely on simpler, fixed antenna deployments within the broad GEO
         footprint’.
(90)     However, the assessment of the qualitative results of the market investigation (i.e.,
         not only the multiple-choice responses for statistical purposes but also their
         submissions) show that most respondents, rather than simply excluding the
         substitution between these two technologies, submit that wide-beam satellites is
         the main relevant technology for media purposes. (107) For example, one
         respondent submitted that ‘Limited footprint: HTS systems operate with multiple,
         narrowly defined spot beams optimized for targeted data transmission. They are
         unsuitable for the widespread broadcast of a TV signal to a broad audience base.
         Technical architecture: TV broadcasting requires uniform coverage of large
         geographical regions (e.g., all of Europe or the DACH region). Widebeam
         capacities offer precisely this. HTS, on the other hand, is designed for selective,
         highly concentrated data capacity […]. Reception infrastructure: HTS systems
         often require specialized, site-specific reception systems with precise alignment,

(105)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.7. The majority of respondents having expressed a view
        excluded that this would change in the coming 5 years. Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.7.2.
(106)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.7.1.
(107)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.7.1. See also Form CO, paras 423, 428.
                                                   19
         whereas widebeam distribution is compatible with millions of existing household
         antennas. Market availability: The current market offering structure clearly
         reflects these differences. HTS is primarily used for two-way data communication,
         not for traditional, unidirectional media distribution’ (translated); (108) another
         respondent submitted that ‘we have limited experience with high-throughput
         satellite (HTS) capacity in the context of one-way media broadcasting’; another
         respondent submitted that ‘only wide beam capacity—typically in Ku-band or C-
         band—is suitable for linear broadcast, DTH, FTA, and cable headend
         distribution’; another respondent submitted that ‘wide-beam is our only option to
         address all households in the respective markets’; another responded submitted
         that ‘we do not currently use HTS but it is in our strategy for future utilisation’;
         another respondent submitted that ‘HTS is used for data purposes and not for
         video encoded channels (sport events, news, etc...)’.
(91)     Another respondent confirmed that ‘wide-beam satellites provide the broad,
         consistent footprint necessary for reaching all DTH, FTA, and cable viewers
         across the UK, a model that has long supported services like [broadcaster]’s
         traditional dish-and-box offering. HTS, with its multiple, focused spot beams for
         higher capacity through frequency reuse, lacks this inherent wide-area coverage’
         (like the other respondents mentioned above). It also added that ‘the strategic shift
         by [broadcaster] away from its dish-based product towards IP-delivered services
         like [OTT services] significantly diminishes the incentive for adopting new
         satellite technology, including HTS, for traditional broadcast distribution […]’.
(92)     The exact market definition can be left open for the purposes of this Decision, as
         the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
         internal market irrespective of whether a possible market of two-way satellite
         capacity from wide-beam satellites or a possible market of two-way satellite
         capacity from HTS satellites is considered. The Commission in this Decision will
         consider the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes) from wide-beam satellites as the narrowest plausible market relevant
         for the Transaction because: (i) wide-beam is the main relevant technology for
         one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) (109) and (ii) there is no overlap
         for HTS technology in Europe. (110)



(108)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.7.1. Original reply: ‘Begrenzte Ausleuchtzonen: HTS-
        Systeme arbeiten mit mehreren, eng begrenzten Spotbeams, die für gezielte Datenübertragung
        optimiert sind. Für die flächendeckende Ausstrahlung eines TV-Signals an eine breite
        Zuschauerbasis sind sie ungeeignet. Technische Architektur: TV-Broadcasting erfordert eine
        gleichmäßige Abdeckung großer geografischer Regionen (z. B. ganz Europa oder DACH).
        Widebeam-Kapazitäten bieten genau das. HTS hingegen ist auf punktuelle, stark gebündelte
        Datenkapazität ausgelegt [...]. Empfangsinfrastruktur: HTS-Systeme erfordern oft spezialisierte,
        standortgebundene Empfangsanlagen mit exakter Ausrichtung, während Widebeam-Verbreitung
        kompatibel mit Millionen bestehender Haushaltsantennen ist. Marktverfügbarkeit: Die heutige
        Angebotsstruktur am Markt reflektiert diese Unterschiede deutlich. HTS wird primär für
        zweiwegige       Datenkommunikation       genutzt,   nicht    für    klassische,  unidirektionale
        Medienverbreitung’.
(109)   More than 96% of satellite transmissions for media purposes used wide-beam satellites in 2024; this
        figure could be as high as 99.7% depending on the classification of transmissions made from
        satellites equipped with both wide-beam and HTS capabilities. Notifying Party’s response of 28
        May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 13, para. 1.1, which in turn refers to an analysis of Lyngsat and
        Novaspace data.
(110)   While Intelsat uses an HTS satellite to distribute media content in Europe, SES does not use HTS
        satellites to distribute media content in Europe. Notifying Party’s response of 28 May 2025 to
        Commission’s RFI 13, para. 1.4.
                                                   20
5.2.2.3.1.3.    Segmentation of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) by
                different frequency bands
(93)     The results of the market investigation are inconclusive on whether the frequency
         band Ku and other frequency brands (such as Ka- and C-band) belong to the same
         relevant product market. By way of background, the market investigation on one-
         way satellite capacity (for media purposes) asked about substitutability between
         the Ku-band and the other frequencies, because Ku-band satellite capacity is most
         commonly used for media distribution in Europe, as explained in paragraphs (44)
         and (61).
(94)     The majority of respondents having expressed a view excluded that Ku-band
         satellite capacity is substitutable with other frequency bands such as Ka- or C-
         band. (111) From a demand-side perspective, several respondents referred to the
         required receiver-side equipment. For example, one respondent submitted that
         ‘substitutability is likely to depend on factors like geographical location, weather
         patterns, equipment costs, and the specific bandwidth requirements of the
         broadcaster’; another respondent explained that ‘the receiver-side equipment
         establishes the band’; and another respondent submitted that another respondent
         submitted that ‘in case of a change from Ku-band to C-band, bigger satellite
         dishes would be needed in the vast majority of satellite households. In case of a
         change from Ku-band to Ka-band, the weather sensitivity would increase
         drastically. Therefore, a change from Ku-band to C- or Ka-band would not be
         reasonable’. (112)
(95)     From a supply side substitutability, several respondents referred to significant
         ground system changes. For example, one competitor submitted that ‘any system
         change will require significant ground system changes as well. One cannot just
         substitute one frequency band for another’; and one customer submitted that ‘the
         use of C-band or Ku-band is dependent on the receiving architecture, which is
         not within our control. It is often region specific’. (113)
(96)     However, the assessment of the qualitative results of the market investigation (i.e.,
         not only the multiple-choice responses for statistical purposes but also their
         submissions) show that most respondents, rather than simply excluding the
         substitution between these frequency bands, submit that the Ku-band is most
         commonly used for media distribution in Europe. For example, one customer
         submitted that ‘Ku band is the most used band for DTH with millions Ku band
         antennas and LNBs’ (114); another customer submitted ‘for DTH satellite
         distribution in Europe, only the Ku-band is used’; another one submitted ‘Ku band
         works well for broadcast contribution, in Europe the majority of the mobile
         uplinks are Ku band, same for the downlinks taking the feeds in’; another
         respondent submitted that ‘[o]ur current use cases so not include Ka or C band.
         Our end-users do not have the devices for these technologies’; another respondent
         submitted that ‘most of the deployed low-noise blocks (LNB) for satellite
         reception are only capable to receive Ku-band’; another respondent submitted
         that ‘Ku-band remains the most widely used and reliable option for one-way
         broadcast services’; another respondent submitted that ‘there are millions of Ku-

(111)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.8. The majority of respondents having expressed a view
        excluded that this would change in the coming 5 years. Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.8.2.

                                                   21
         band dishes in place but only a handful of Ka or C-band dishes. [We] require[]
         millions’; another respondent submitted that ‘the Ku band is widely spread and
         used for public distribution and non-private contributions (FTA)’. (115)
(97)     This is also confirmed by the fact that, in response to the question whether
         frequency bands Ka-band or C-band (other than Ku-band) could be considered
         separate markets, the majority of respondents having expressed a view responded
         in the negative. (116)
(98)     The exact market definition can be left open for the purposes of this Decision, as
         the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
         internal market irrespective of whether narrower potential markets for each
         individual frequency or a broader market comprising all frequency bands is
         defined. The Commission in this Decision will consider the Ku-band capacity
         market as the narrowest plausible market relevant for the Transaction because: (i)
         Ku-band is the main frequency band for one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes); (117) and (ii) for each of Ka-band or C-band there is no overlap between
         the Parties (118).

5.2.2.3.1.4.    Segmentation of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) by
                different orbital locations
(99)     The results of the market investigation are mixed on whether different orbital
         locations are different segments within the market for the supply of one-way
         satellite capacity (for media purposes).
(100)    From a demand-side perspective, the Commission’s market investigation tested
         whether the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) is
         substitutable between different orbital locations, based on their product
         characteristics, prices and intended use. The results of the market investigation
         are mixed: (119)
         (a)    A majority excluded the substitutability between different orbital locations.
                For example, one respondent submitted that ‘when our affiliates and ground
                viewers have invested in installing and pointing an antenna towards a given
                satellite position, we cannot be sure that they will agree to migrate to a new
                satellite position if necessary, especially since the value of a position also
                depends also on the neighboring services’. (120)
         (b)    However, a significant minority confirmed such substitutability. For
                example, one respondent submitted that ‘satellites at different orbital
                locations could be partially substitutable with each other, dependent on



(115)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.8.1.
(116)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.8.3.

        Europe for 2021-24 for each distribution method: these shares range from 96.9% for FTA in 2022
        up to 100% for DTH in 2022 to 2024. According to the Notifying Party, any market shares for Ku-
        band would not materially differ from the other market shares for the supply of one-way satellite
        capacity across all frequency bands. Notifying Party’s response of 27 May 2025 to the Commission
        RFI 12, paras 2.1., 2.2.
(118)   Neither SES nor Intelsat used Ka-band or C-band for any of the Media channel transmission
        distributed using their Satellite Capacity in Europe in 2024. Notifying Party’s response of 27 May
        2025 to the Commission RFI 12, question 2, para. 2.4.
(119)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.4.
(120)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.4.1.
                                                   22
                how close the positions are and whether the two different positions could
                still provide the same coverage for a geographical area’.
(101)    From a supply-side perspective, the Commission’s market investigation tested
         whether SNOs able to switch easily and quickly between different orbital
         locations. The results of the market investigation are mixed: (121)
         (a)    A majority excluded that SNOs can switch between different orbital
                locations. For example, one respondent submitted that ‘when our affiliates
                and ground viewers have invested in installing and pointing an antenna
                towards a given satellite position, we cannot be sure that they will agree to
                migrate to a new satellite position if necessary, especially since the value of
                a position also depends also on the neighboring services’. (122)
         (b)    However, a minority confirmed such switch substitutability. For example,
                one respondent submitted that ‘generally, SNOs can easily and quickly
                switch their ground stations to different orbital locations in support of
                satellite network operations. SNOs also have the ability control the
                satellite’s orbital locations’.
(102)    The exact market definition can be left open, as the Transaction does not raise
         serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market irrespective of
         whether narrower potential markets for each individual orbital location or a
         broader market comprising all orbital locations is defined. In any case, the Parties
         do not overlap in any orbital location (paragraph (40)). Therefore, for market
         definition purposes, the Decision will consider the market of the one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes) for all orbital locations as the narrowest plausible
         market relevant for the Transaction.

5.2.2.3.1.5.    Segmentation of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) by
                distribution types such as DTH, FTA or cable
(103)    The results of the market investigation are mixed on the question whether the
         supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) for each of DTH, FTA
         and/or cable distribution constitute separate markets.
(104)    From a demand-side perspective, the Commission’s market investigation tested
         whether the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) for DTH,
         FTA and/or cable distribution is substitutable between them, based on their
         product characteristics, prices and intended use. The results are mixed. (123)
         (a)    About half of the respondents having expressed a view excluded the
                substitutability between DTH, FTA and cable. For example, one respondent
                submitted that ‘satellite DTH has some unique characteristics which cannot
                be replaced by Cable delivery in some circumstances. Houses in remote
                locations, on mountains or other areas where it does not make commercial
                sense to deploy a cable network rely on Satellite’; another respondent
                submitted that ‘DTH targets individual households with specific satellite
                alignment, while cable distribution involves signal delivery to network
                headends, making the capacity use case and configuration fundamentally
                different’; yet another respondent submitted that ‘the DTH, FTA and/or
                Cable distribution broadcast modes are theoretically substituable from


(121)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.11.
(122)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.11.1.
(123)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question C.A.A.3.
                                                  23
                each other but in practice it is rendered difficult by the burdensome need to
                change the orbital location and size of the dishes’. (124)
         (b)    The other approximate half of the respondents having expressed a view
                confirmed such the substitutability. For example, one respondent submitted
                that ‘one-way satellite capacity can be used simultaneously for both, DTH
                and as a feed for cable distribution (or other means of distribution). FTA
                only means that a channel is unencrypted, regardless whether it is
                distributed via satellite, cable, IPTV, etc.’; another respondent submitted
                that ‘the fundamental requirement is the reliable transmission of a
                broadcast signal from the uplink facility to the satellite and then down to
                the receiving infrastructure. Whether the final destination is a DTH receiver
                in a home, a Free-to-Air set-top box, or the headend of a cable network, the
                one-way satellite link serves the same primary purpose: delivering the
                linear channel feed’. (125)
(105)    From a supply-side perspective, the Commission’s market investigation tested
         whether SNOs are able to switch easily and quickly between the supply of one-
         way satellite capacity for each of DTH, FTA and/or cable distribution. The results
         of the market investigation are mixed. (126)
         (a)    About half of the respondents having expressed a view excluded that SNOs
                can switch between DTH, FTA and cable. For example, one respondent
                submitted that ‘DTH/FTA: both services usually count with a big volume of
                end user base. The change of capacity in this segment will suppose high
                costs (millions of Euros) and will be slow (months/years). Cable
                distribution: the number of satellite receivers are low compared to
                DTH/FTA. The change can be done without big investment specially
                between orbital locations that are already dedicated to video distribution’;
                another respondent submitted ‘this can be done, but it is subject to
                equipment constraints related to the satellite frequencies’.
         (b)    The other approximate half of the respondents having expressed a view
                confirmed such switching possibility for SNOs between DTH, FTA and
                cable. For example, one respondent submitted that ‘on a same satellite,
                transponders can be leased either to DTH operators (both pay-per-view or
                FTA) but also to cable operators. In the former case, programs are
                broadcast to the end-users terminals. In the latter case, programs are
                feeding the cable head-ends of the cable operator which are equipped with
                large dishes. If a customer (DTH, FTA or cable operator) ceased to use the
                leased transponder, the SNO could lease the freed capacity to another
                operator (DTH, FTA or cable)’.
(106)    Therefore, as the market investigation’s results are mixed, the exact market
         definition can be left open for the purposes of this Decision. In any case, the
         Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal
         market when assessing the plausible markets corresponding to each of DTH, FTA
         and/or cable distribution.

                                                     24
5.2.2.3.1.6.    Conclusion on the relevant product market for the supply of one-way
                satellite capacity (for media purposes)
(107)    The relevant product market is the market for the supply of one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes).
(108)    The question whether this market can be segmented by: (i) orbit (GEO or LEO);
         (ii) satellite type (HTS or wide-beam), (iii) frequency band (Ku-, Ka-, C-band),
         (iv) orbital locations or (v) distribution types (DTH, FTA, cable), can be left open
         as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
         internal market under any plausible market definition.

5.2.2.3.2.      Market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity
(109)    With regard to the market for two-way satellite capacity, the market investigation
         tested whether there is a need to segment the market between: (i) GEO and
         LEO/NGSO capacity; (ii) traditional/wide-beam and HTS/newer technology
         capacity; (iii) Ku-, Ka- and C-band capacity, and (iv) industry segments (other
         than media).

5.2.2.3.2.1.    Segmentation between GEO and LEO two-way satellite capacity
(110)    The market investigation results were inconclusive as to whether two-way
         satellite capacity from GEO satellites and two-way satellite capacity from LEO
         satellites (such as Starlink or Eutelsat’s OneWeb) belong to the same relevant
         product market.
(111)    From a demand-side perspective, the majority of respondents submitted that two-
         way satellite capacity from GEO satellites is substitutable with two-way satellite
         capacity from LEO satellites, based on product characteristics, prices and
         intended uses. (127) Some of these respondents also submitted that the current
         rapid entry of LEO constellations would show that the GEO and NGSO satellites
         cater to the same customer needs, such as broadband internet, telecommunication
         and in-flight connectivity. (128)
(112)    From a supply-side perspective, the vast majority of SNOs having expressed a
         view indicated that they could not switch easily from supplying two-way satellite
         capacity from a GEO satellite to supplying two-way satellite capacity from a LEO
         satellite (and vice versa) in a short period of time and without incurring significant
         costs. (129)
(113)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that the exact market definition
         can be left open for the purposes of this Decision, as the Transaction does not
         raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market irrespective of
         whether a narrower market comprising solely GEO capacity, or a broader market
         comprising both GEO and LEO capacity, is defined.

5.2.2.3.2.2.    Segmentation between HTS and wide-beam two-way satellite capacity
(114)    The results of the market investigation were mixed on whether two-way satellite
         capacity from HTS satellites and two-way satellite capacity from wide-beam
         satellites belong to the same relevant product market.



(127)   Replies to Q2 (Two-way capacity), question C.A.5.
(128)   Replies to Q2 (Two-way capacity), question C.A.5.1.
(129)   Replies to Q2 (Two-way capacity), question C.A.6.
                                                 25
(115)    From a demand-side perspective, more respondents having expressed a view
         considered that two-way satellite capacity from HTS satellites is substitutable
         with two-way satellite capacity from wide-beam satellites. (130) Some of these
         respondents stated that the same customer needs are served by both satellite types,
         and that they observe this substitution currently in the market. (131)
(116)    Respondents who do not consider that HTS and wide-beam two-way satellite
         capacity are substitutable noted that HTS satellites are generally more efficient,
         provide higher bit rates and performances and are less costly when compared to
         wide-beam satellites. In view of these advantages, several respondents – despite
         initially indicating that HTS and wide-beam capacity are not generally
         substitutable – agreed that one way substitution is plausible and has actually
         started taking place. In this respect one respondent submitted that ‘it is ‘easier to
         switch from wide beam to HTS than from HTS to wide beam’. Another respondent
         sated that ‘substituting HTS for wide-beam capacity for two-way services is
         reasonable and potentially favorable as well. Conversely, substituting wide-beam
         services for HTS services is unlikely to meet the capacity requirements for a given
         coverage area’. The same responded also added that ‘[w]ide-beam services are
         predominately used for broadcast services. For two-way services, most MNOs
         have adopted HTS services for their customer service requirements, but it is not
         uncommon for SNOs to have single satellites that provide both wide-beam and
         HTS coverages’. Another respondent submitted that there exists ‘[o]ne-sided
         interchangeability – transition from wide beam to HTS’. Another respondent
         noted that ‘[m]arket is migrating from wide-beam to HTS’. (132)
(117)    From a supply-side perspective, most SNOs having expressed a view considered
         that they could not switch easily from supplying two-way satellite capacity from
         a wide-beam satellite to supplying two-way satellite capacity from an HTS
         satellite (and vice versa) in a short period of time and without incurring significant
         costs. (133) One of these respondents explained that for an SNO to switch between
         satellite types would imply significant cost for inter alia launching new satellites,
         investments in ground infrastructure and obtaining new licenses. (134)
(118)    In any event, the Commission considers that the exact market definition can be
         left open for the purposes of this Decision, as the Transaction does not raise
         serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market irrespective of
         whether a possible market of two-way satellite capacity from wide-beam satellites
         or a possible market of two-way satellite capacity from HTS satellites is
         considered.

5.2.2.3.2.3.    Segmentation of two-way satellite capacity by different frequency bands
(119)    The results of the market investigation were mixed on whether it would be
         appropriate to segment the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity by
         different frequency bands such as Ku-, Ka- and C-band.
(120)    From a demand-side perspective, in response to the question whether two-way
         satellite capacity is substitutable between different frequency bands, about one
         third submitted ‘yes’, about one third submitted ‘no’, and about one third

         and Ka-bands are closer substitutes to each other, compared to Ka/Ku-band versus

         cabin communications and therefore not interchangeable with either Ku or
