         and Ku-band) can achieve identical uplink and downlink speeds for a given set of
         beams simply by altering any of the performance factors (e.g., using larger
         ground equipment)’. (136)
(121)    From a supply-side perspective, a vast majority of the SNOs who expressed a
         view do not consider that they could switch easily from supplying two-way
         satellite capacity in a particular frequency band to supplying two-way satellite
         capacity in a different frequency band, in a short period of time and without
         incurring significant costs. (137)
(122)    In any event, the Commission considers that the exact market definition can be
         left open for the purposes of this Decision, as the Transaction does not raise
         serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market irrespective of
         whether narrower potential markets for each individual frequency bands or a
         broader market comprising all frequency bands is defined.

5.2.2.3.2.4.    Segmentation of two-way satellite capacity by downstream industry
                segments/end-uses
(123)    The market investigation confirmed that the market for the supply of two-way
         satellite capacity should not be segmented based on the downstream industry
         segment/end-use. The vast majority of respondents considered that two-way
         satellite capacity is fungible and can be used interchangeably across a range of
         industry segments/end-uses. (138)
(124)    For example, one respondent submitted that ‘all SNOs may allocate capacity to
         the different industry segments. SSPs and resellers/VARs are specialised in
         serving one or more particular industries or customer segments for a number of
         reasons’. Another respondent submitted that ‘GEO is designed so that different
         types of satellite services can be provided across the different industry segments’.
         A further respondent stated that ‘two-way satellite capacity is generally fungible
         across different industry segments. Satellite capacity is usually provided to
         various SSPs offering satellite services in different industry segments. However,
         some of these SSPs might be specialized in one industry segment only, and some
         applications have specific requirements that can limit the fungibility’. (139)
(125)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that the market for the supply of
         two-way satellite capacity by SNOs should not be segmented based on the
         downstream industry segment.

5.2.2.3.2.5.     Conclusion on the relevant product market for the supply of two-way
                 satellite capacity
(126)     Considering the above, the Commission concludes that the relevant product
          market is the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity across different
          industries (other than media).
(127)     The question whether this market can be segmented by (i) orbit (GEO or LEO);
          (ii) satellite type (HTS or wide-beam) or (iii) frequency band (Ka-, Ku-, C-band),
          can be left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
          compatibility with the internal market under any plausible market definition.

5.2.3.    Geographic market definition

5.2.3.1. Market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes)

5.2.3.1.1.       Commission’s precedents
(128)     The Commission considered the market for the supply of satellite capacity to be
          worldwide in scope in its Viasat/Inmarsat decision. (140) However, that decision
          dealt with two-way satellite communications, because one of the merging parties
          was not active in the provision of one-way satellite communications (as noted in
          footnote 66).
(129)     The Commission considered a regional scope, and not the EEA as a whole, in its
          previous decisions dealing with satellite capacity for media purposes (‘satellite
          transponder capacity’ for broadcasting and other telecommunication services, as
          described in paragraph (47)):
          (a)    In BC Partners/United Group, the Commission, while it ultimately left the
                 market open, it considered the region ‘Central and Eastern Europe’ (‘CEE’)
                 because ‘the information gathered during the market investigation
                 confirmed the regional scope of the supply of satellite transponder capacity.
                 Indeed, the satellites covering Slovenia and Croatia have a supranational
                 footprint, which also covers neighbouring states of Central and Eastern
                 Europe. Their position is stronger or weaker as per territory, depending on
                 the strength of the beam and on the number of dishes pointed towards them’.
                 (141)
          (b)    In NENT/Telenor/JV, the Commission, while it ultimately left the market
                 open, considered the Nordics region, or Nordics and CEE region, because
                 ‘the results of the market investigation confirmed that it is relevant to look
                 at satellites’ combined footprint from an orbital location (technical reach)
                 and the location of the customer base (number of dishes pointed towards an
                 orbital location)’ and, on this basis, ‘the information gathered confirms the
                 regional scope (Nordics, or Nordics and CEE region)’.(142)

5.2.3.1.2.       Notifying Party’s views
(130)     The Notifying Party submits that that the appropriate geographic scope for the
          supply of satellite capacity is at least Europe-wide or global in scope, (143)

(142)    Commission decision of 30 April 2020 in case M.9604 - NENT/Telenor/JV, para. 85.

         because: (i) satellite systems are typically designed to cover all or most of Europe
         (and often stretch beyond the continent to cover adjacent regions or provide global
         coverage) and the signal on a GEO satellite can be received by anyone within the
         beam coverage area using the right equipment; (144) (ii) there are a large number
         of competitors with global or multi-regional coverage; (145) and (iii) the location
         of the customer itself does not have an appreciable impact on the costs of supply,
         and more generally distance-related factors and trade flows are not relevant in the
         industry. (146)
(131)    The Notifying Party also submits that it would be not appropriate for the purposes
         of the assessment of the Proposed Transaction to read the previous Commission
         decision NENT/Telenor/JV as indicating a narrow geographic market for satellite
         capacity. (147)

5.2.3.1.3.      Commission’s assessment
(132)    In the market investigation, the Commission tested generally which conditions of
         competition are relevant to distinguish one geographic market from another
         geographic market. In addition, for the supply of one-way capacity (for media
         purposes), the Commission tested at which geographic level (i) the conditions of
         competition are largely similar/homogenous, (ii) contracts are negotiated and (iii)
         whether SNOs are able to switch supply easily and quickly between different
         geographic regions.
(133)    To the question at which geographic level are the conditions of competition (for
         example, prices, identity of suppliers, their market strength, customer’s
         preferences, satellite technical reach, number of dishes pointed) are largely
         similar/homogenous for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes), respondents gave very different replies but the option that was picked
         most often was ‘regional’. (148)
(134)    The above-mentioned respondents having replied ‘regional’, identify different
         geographic regions within the EEA, sometimes describing linguistic areas or,
         more often, broader regions corresponding to (i) Western Europe, (ii) Eastern
         Europe, (iii) CEE [Central and Eastern Europe] and (iv) Nordics or Nordic
         Europe. (149) These regions may correspond to the satellite technical reach, as set
         out in paragraph (136). In addition, these regions were possible geographic
         markets in the Commission’s previous decisions (as set out in paragraph (129))
         and/or were referred in the Parties’ internal documents. (150)
(135)    As an example of the above-mentioned ‘regional’ respondents, a customer
         submitted that ‘GEO satellites have a regional coverage encompassing different
         countries. For example, Astra 1 covers central Europe, including France,
         Germany, Italy, and Spain, while a different constellation covers the UK. Satellite
         operators, such as SES, Intelsat and Eutelsat have offerings covering different

         wide-beams used for broadcasting are typically multi-thousand kilometers wide,

         contracts are traditionally with regional media broadcasters that purchase
         capacity corresponding to the transnational coverage of a GEO asset or for
         foreign communities outside their home country - SNOs compete to control the
         key orbital slots (aka ‘hot spots’ where massive numbers of dishes are pointing
         to) providing efficient coverage at regional level. However, in some cases, the
         ‘national’ level is more relevant…’ (152). That same respondent submitted also
         that ‘‘… however, in some cases, the ‘national’ level is more relevant, for
         instance: SES has long dominated the German market through long-standing
         deals with numerous German broadcasters, establishing itself as the prime TV
         neighborhood in the country. In Italy, the market is more specialized, with Sky
         Italia securing multiple agreements with operators, shaping a unique and
         competitive landscape’.
(136)    When asked to rank the factors to determine conditions of competition to
         distinguish one geographic market from another geographic market, most
         respondents ranked in the following order: (i) satellite technical reach; (ii) number
         of dishes pointed towards an orbital location; (iii) price for satellite capacity; (iv)
         identity of providers of satellite capacity; (v) different preferences of customers
         procuring satellite capacity. (153) This explains the fact that the above-mentioned
         ‘regional’ respondents may refer to the satellite technical reach (paragraph (134)).
(137)    The possible regional scope of the relevant market is also confirmed by the result
         of the market investigation on the geographic area based on which the contracts
         for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) are negotiated.
         Most respondents replied ‘regional’. (154) For example, one respondent submitted
         that they ‘negotiate content rights on a national basis and [they] aim to use the
         same satellite capacity and orbital location to address different countries on a
         regional basis’. (155)
(138)    A minority of respondents submitted that the market would be ‘EEA’. (156)

(156)   For example, one respondent submitted that ‘competitive conditions for one-way satellite capacities
        are largely homogeneous. … Provider structure: The most important SNOs (e.g., SES, Eutelsat)

        in the EEA are based on a common European price level, depending on transponder performance,
        range, and frequency band – not on national borders. Technical framework: Satellite signals via
        Ku-band wide-beam technology provide uniform coverage throughout Europe. TV viewers in
        multiple EEA countries receive programs via the same satellites, without any country-specific
        differences in technology. Regulatory environment: Within the EEA, comparable legal frameworks

         ‘national’. (157) Combining the above-mentioned ‘regional’ and ‘national’
         respondents, the Commission finds that the market investigation results suggest
         that the geographic scope of the relevant market is likely broader than national
         but narrower than the EEA.

5.2.3.1.4.       Conclusion on the relevant geographic market for the supply of one-way
                 satellite capacity (for media purposes)
(139)    For the purposes of this Decision, the exact geographic market definition can be
         left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility
         with the internal market under any plausible geographic market definition.
(140)    Therefore, in this Decision the Commission will conduct its assessment on the
         basis of both EEA-wide and regional markets, namely Western Europe, Eastern
         Europe, CEE and Nordic Europe (paragraph (134)). For clarity, Section 6.3.1
         market share tables refer to Western Eastern and Eastern Europe, because there
         are no affected markets (whose meaning is recalled in footnote 253) on the basis
         of the market shares for CEE and Nordic Europe (paragraph (229)) on any
         plausible product market definition.

5.2.3.2. Market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity

5.2.3.2.1.       Commission’s precedents
(141)    The Commission considered the market for the supply of two-way satellite
         capacity to be worldwide in scope in its Viasat/Inmarsat decision. (158) That
         decision dealt with two-way satellite communications, because one of the



        Kapazitäten grenzüberschreitend innerhalb des EWR an. Die Auswahl an Dienstleistern und deren

          merging parties was not active in the provision of one-way satellite

5.2.3.2.2.       Notifying Party’s views
(142)     As outlined above, the Notifying Party submits that the appropriate geographic
          scope for the supply of satellite capacity (160) is at least Europe-wide or global in
          scope, (161) because: (i) satellite systems are typically designed to cover all or
          most of Europe (and often stretch beyond the continent to cover adjacent regions
          or provide global coverage) and the signal on a GEO satellite can be received by
          anyone within the beam coverage area using the right equipment; (162) (ii) there
          are a large number of competitors with global or multi-regional coverage; (163)
          and (iii) the location of the customer itself does not have an appreciable impact
          on the costs of supply, and more generally distance-related factors and trade flows
          are not relevant in the industry. (164)

5.2.3.2.3.       Commission’s assessment
(143)     The market investigation indicated that the geographic scope of the market for the
          supply of two-way satellite capacity is worldwide or EEA-wide.
(144)     The majority of respondents having expressed a view consider that the geographic
          level where the conditions of competition for the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity (e.g., prices, customers’ preferences, number and identity of suppliers,
          their market strength) are largely similar/homogenous, is the worldwide or EEA-
          wide level. (165) Furthermore, the majority of respondents having expressed a
          view confirmed that they negotiate contracts for the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity at the worldwide level. (166)

5.2.3.2.4.       Conclusion on the relevant geographic market for the supply of two-way
                 satellite capacity
(145)     For the purposes of this Decision, the exact geographic market definition can be
          left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility
          with the internal market under any plausible geographic market definition.
(146)     Therefore, the Commission will conduct its assessment on the basis of both global
          and EEA-wide plausible geographic market definitions.

5.3.      Market for the supply of satellite services

5.3.1.    The Parties’ activities
(147)     As vertically integrated SNOs/SSPs, both Parties also offer satellite services
          combining satellite capacity with additional services such as fibre connectivity,
          user terminals and customer support. (167) The Parties provide two-way satellite

         aircrafts. (169) Intelsat offers satellite services to SSPs (e.g., […]), as well as

         lines, such as […]), but not to SSPs. (176) SES offers satellite services employing
         broadband frequencies and using wide-beam and HTS satellites. (177)
(150)    Government. Both Parties provide satellite services to customers in the
         government sector. (178) Government satellite services are broadly similar to those
         in other segments, such as fixed data and maritime, but they are distinguished by
         particular government demands to ensure reliability and security through inter
         alia stronger service level commitments, jamming, enhanced resiliency and
         encryption. (179) SES provides satellite services in particular to […] but also
         services […]. (180) Intelsat is active globally with […]. (181) The Parties employ
         broadband frequencies using wide-beam and HTS satellites.

         from remote locations to core networks. Second, the Parties offer trunking
         services using a satellite link to connect rural areas without fiber access to the
         internet or to serve as a backup to terrestrial networks. (184) Third, the Parties offer

5.3.2.    Product market definition

5.3.2.1. Commission’s precedents
(152)     In Astrium Holding/Vizada Group and Apax Partners/Telenor Satellite Services,
          the Commission identified a separate market for the supply of satellite
          connectivity services at ‘wholesale level’, i.e., by SSPs to downstream customers
          (large end customers and resellers). (187) The Commission considered that the
          market for the supply of satellite connectivity services could be segmented
          between (i) military and commercial satellite services; and (ii) one-way or two-
          way satellite services. (188) Further, the Commission considered that the market
          for satellite communication services could be segmented based on the end use
          (e.g., whether connectivity is used for maritime or aviation applications), but
          ultimately left the market definition open as regards a possible segmentation by
          end use. (189)
(153)     In Viasat/Inmarsat, the Commission concluded that there is a segmentation based
          on the level of supply chain (between SNOs, SSPs and VARs) and, therefore,
          there exists a separate market for the supply of satellite connectivity services by
          SSPs, which can be segmented according to the industry of application (e.g.,
          aviation, maritime). (190) Furthermore, the Commission considered possible
          segmentations within the aviation sector including based on: (i) type of customer
          (i.e., commercial or business), and (ii) GEO- and LEO-based in-flight
          connectivity (‘IFC’) solutions. The Commission concluded that there is an overall
          market for the supply of broadband IFC services to commercial aviation
          customers, including both Ka- and Ku- bands and GEO- and LEO-based
          solutions, for short-haul and long-haul flights. The question of whether that
          market should be further segmented according to whether the IFC services are
          based on satellite technology, air-to-ground (‘ATG’) technology or hybrid
          (satellite and ATG) was left open. (191)
(154)     The Commission has not previously addressed in its precedent decisions the
          possible distinctions between fixed data, government and maritime industries
          within the market for the supply of satellite connectivity services by SSPs.

5.3.2.2. Notifying Party’s views
(155)     The Notifying Party is of the view that the segmentation based on the level of the
          supply chain (between SNOs, SSPs and VARs) in Viasat/Inmarsat (192) does not

                                                   34
         properly reflect the supply chain in industry segments other than aviation because
         the supply chains would not be as linear as in aviation. (193) However, the
         Notifying Party submits that the supply of satellite services is broadly analogous
         to the supply of satellite connectivity services by SSPs as described in the
         Viasat/Inmarsat decision.
(156)    The Notifying Party considers that the market for the supply of satellite services
         by SSPs constitutes a separate market which should be further segmented by
         industry segments: aviation, maritime, government and fixed data. (194)
(157)    The Notifying Party submits that the various industry segments of satellite
         services (aviation, maritime, government and fixed data) should not be further
         sub-segmented by orbit (particularly GEO and NGSO/LEO), satellite type
         (particularly HTS and wide-beam) or frequency band (namely Ka-, Ku- and C-
         band) for the same reasons as outlined in Section 5.2.2.2. for satellite
         capacity. (195)
(158)    Aviation. In the aviation industry, the Notifying Party agrees with the precedent
         in Viasat/Inmarsat in so far as there is an overall market for the supply of
         broadband IFC services to commercial aviation customers, including both Ka-
         and Ku- bands and GEO- and LEO-based solutions, for short-haul and long-haul
         flights. Also, the Notifying Party is not opposed to the distinction between IFC
         services provided to commercial aviation customers on the one hand and to
         business aviation customers on the other hand. (196) Regarding a further market
         segmentation based on technology, the Notifying Party submits that for European,
         short-haul flights there should be no segmentation of IFC services based on
         whether they use ATG or satellite technology because they are fully
         interchangeable. Nonetheless, the Notifying Party submits that for long-haul
         flights ATG is not an alternative because of a lack of ATG infrastructure over
         large land masses and oceans. (197)
(159)    Maritime. The Notifying Party submits that the maritime sector should not be
         further sub-segmented given the considerable supply-side substitutability
         between potential sub-segments such as cruise operators and commercial shipping
         lines. (198)
(160)    Government. The Notifying Party states that no further segmentation is required
         in the government sector. From a demand side perspective, the product
         characteristics in terms of service level requirements for strong reliability and
         heightened security are prevalent across different types of government
         applications such as aircraft, naval, land or command bases. In the Notifying
         Party’s view, these typical service level requirements are also a determining factor
         in pricing satellite services in the government sector as heightened security
         demands result in higher cost for equipment and services. (199) Further, the
         contracting party in the government sector is often a centralised body aggregating
         the demands for different government use cases. (200) From a supply-side
         perspective, the Notifying Party notes that the Parties’ main competitors like

                                             35
         Viasat/Inmarsat and Eutelsat/OneWeb offer their satellite services across
         different applications in the government sector, like maritime, aviation and
         land. (201) Regarding satellite services for the military, the Notifying Party infers
         that satellite services for military applications are part of the same market as
         government satellite services. Military customers often use the same commercial
         frequencies (Ka-, Ku-and C-band) and not only the specific military frequencies
         (X-band and military Ka-band) implying a high degree of demand-side
         substitutability according to the Notifying Party. (202)
(161)    Fixed data. The Notifying Party holds the view that the fixed data industry should
         not be further segmented. (203) First, the Notifying Party submits that there is
         demand-side substitutability between different applications such as backhauling,
         trunking and enterprise connectivity in terms of product characteristics, prices,
         functionalities and intended use. (204) The common denominator of the different
         applications is providing satellite connectivity between two fixed points. While
         the Notifying Party concedes that the volume demanded for cellular backhauling
         and enterprise connectivity is usually lower than for trunking, it submits that
         individual customer requirements across different applications are the decisive
         factor to determine the service provided. (205) Further, the Notifying Party submits
         that product characteristics such as upload speeds, latency and availability
         commitments are similar throughout different applications (trunking, backhauling
         and enterprise connectivity). (206) Second, the Notifying Party states that there is
         supply-side substitutability between different types of satellite services in the
         fixed data industry considering the ability of suppliers to switch supply between
         them without incurring significant cost. (207) According to the Notifying Party,
         switching is easy for suppliers because the technical requirements for
         backhauling, trunking, and enterprise connectivity are similar, such that no
         significant adjustment in assets, investment or risk are needed. (208)

5.3.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(162)    The market investigation confirmed that the supply of two-way satellite capacity
         by SNOs constitutes a separate market from the supply of satellite services by
         SSPs within the satellite communications vertical chain. The majority of market
         participants active in each of the aviation, maritime, government and fixed data
         industries agree that the distinction between satellite capacity and satellite
         services is still relevant. (209)

                                                    36
5.3.2.3.1.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation industry
(163)    The majority of respondents active in the aviation industry that expressed a view
         considered that satellite services used for aviation, maritime, government and
         fixed data applications are only substitutable with one another to a limited extent
         or not substitutable at all, based on product characteristics, prices and intended
         use. (210) Some of these respondents pointed to specific requirements in the
         aviation industry such as regulatory requirements, high quality and tailor-made
         solutions ensuring the technological capabilities to function during flights moving
         at speeds and altitudes unlike in other industries. (211)
(164)    From a supply-side perspective, only a minority of suppliers in the aviation
         industry who expressed a view consider that they could switch easily from the
         supply of satellite services in one industry segment to supplying another industry
         segment. (212)
(165)    Based on the above, the Commission considers that the results of the market
         investigation do not justify a departure from its previous practice, that there is a
         separate market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation industry.
(166)    As regards a further segmentation of satellite services supplied to aviation
         customers based on the technology used, the vast majority of respondents having
         expressed a view considered that satellite services based on ATG technology and
         satellite services based on satellite technology are not substitutable in terms of
         product characteristics, prices and intended use. (213) These respondents noted
         that ATG technology does not effectively cover flights over water and that
         connectivity speeds and data rates are different. (214) Similarly, the vast majority
         of suppliers having expressed a view submitted that they would not be able to
         switch easily from supplying satellite services based on ATG technology to
         supplying satellite services based on satellite technology (and vice versa), in a
         short period of time and without incurring significant costs. (215)
(167)    Based on the above, the Commission considers that it is likely that there are
         separate markets for the supply of satellite services in the aviation industry based
         on satellite technology on the one hand and ATG technology on the other hand.
         However, for the purposes of this Decision, the question whether a distinction
         should be drawn between satellite and ATG technologies can left open because
         the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
         internal market under neither potential market segmentation.
(168)    Regarding the potential further segmentation between satellite services for
         commercial aviation customers and for business aviation customers, the results of
         the market investigation have been inconclusive. Respondents were split on
         whether satellite services for commercial aviation customers and for business

        hardware interoperability with the satellite network (e.g., modem), avionics support, complex
        commercial arrangements including sponsorships, regular software updates, monitoring of
        performance, service level agreements, marketing support, passenger portal, integration with
        airline and other databases, economics, market access, etc.’ (reply to Q3 (Aviation), question
        C.A.2).

(214)   Replies to Q3 (Aviation), question C.A.6.1.
(215)   Replies to Q3 (Aviation), question C.A.7.
                                                    37
         aviation customers constitute a single or separate markets. (216) Market
         participants who did not consider these potential subsegments to be
         interchangeable pointed out inter alia that different terminals are needed for the
         respective use case, differences in billing (usually a fixed monthly fee per aircraft
         in business aviation as opposed to a pay-per-use system in commercial aviation),
         and performance monitoring which is more stringent in commercial aviation. (217)
         On the other hand, respondents who considered these potential subsegments to be
         interchangeable note that hardware requirements for aviation are usually the same
         for business and commercial aviation and that the requirements of end users in
         terms of high throughput, full coverage and low latency are the same. (218)
(169)    In any event, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this Decision,
         the question whether the market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation
         industry should be segmented between commercial and business aviation
         customers can remain open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts with
         the internal market under neither potential market segmentation.
(170)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that, for the purposes of this
         Decision, there is likely a separate market for the supply of satellite services to
         customers in the aviation industry. However, for the purposes of the Decision, the
         exact product market definition can be left open, including the question whether
         this potential market should be further segmented between commercial and
         business aviation customers and between satellite-based and ATG-based
         technology, because the Transaction would not raise doubts as to its compatibility
         with the internal market under any plausible product market definition.

5.3.2.3.2.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry
(171)    All respondents active in the maritime industry that expressed a view considered
         that satellite services used for maritime, aviation, government and fixed data
         applications are only substitutable with one another to a limited extent or not
         substitutable at all, based on product characteristics, prices and intended use. (219)
         One of the customers stated that ‘each segment has unique requirements, such as
         reliable connectivity in remote oceanic regions for maritime services or secure
         communications for government applications. Pricing also varies significantly,
         with specialized services often coming at a premium. Additionally, the intended
         use of these services differs, with maritime services supporting navigation and
         safety at sea, aviation services providing in-flight connectivity, fixed data services
         catering to broadband internet, and government services focusing on defense and
         emergency response’. (220)
(172)    From a supply-side perspective, the supplier who explained its view considered
         that switching as a SSP from the supply of satellite services for other industries to
         supply for the maritime industry requires ‘extensive capital investment’ for
         example to replace terminals and for installation cost. (221)
(173)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that, for the purposes of this
         Decision, there is likely a separate market for the supply of satellite services to
         customers in the maritime industry. However, for the purposes of the Decision,

         would not raise doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market even under

5.3.2.3.3.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the government sector
(174)    The feedback to the market investigation from market participants active in the
         government sector was mixed as to whether satellite services used for
         government, aviation, maritime and fixed data applications are substitutable with
         one another.
(175)    There was a three-way split between respondents regarding the question whether
         satellite services for the different industry segments are interchangeable, based on
         product characteristics, prices and intended use. (222) The relatively largest
         proportion was of the opinion that satellite services are indeed interchangeable
         across all the different industry segments. However, two smaller groups of
         respondents did not agree or considered that satellite services are only to a certain
         extent substitutable across the different industry segments. (223) One proponent of
         substitutability of satellite services across industry segments argued that
         customers increasingly ask for integrated solutions with similar expectations in
         terms of availability, quality, mobility, and security across industries. Another
         respondent stated that ‘[t]he Satellite Services are suitable and sustainable in all
         segments’. (224) By contrast, respondents who do not consider that satellite
         services are substitutable across industry segments pointed out that each industry
         segment has specific requirements regarding security, user terminals (modems
         and antennas) and regulation. Further, respondents submitted that the technology
         used in the government sector is not compatible with other industry segments
         because of the need for military grade technology and operational excellence. (225)
(176)    From a supply-side perspective, the majority of suppliers in the government sector
         having expressed a view indicated that they are not able to switch easily from the
         supply of satellite services for other industries to supply satellite services for the
         government sector, in a short period of time and without incurring significant
         costs. (226) These respondents submitted that switching to the government sector
         requires time, investment and diligent planning due to heightened security
         requirements. (227)
(177)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that, for the purposes of this
         Decision, there is likely a separate market for the supply of satellite services to
         customers in the government sector. However, for the purposes of this Decision,
         the exact product market definition can be left open because the Transaction
         would not raise doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any
         plausible product market definition.

5.3.2.3.4.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry
(178)    The majority of respondents active in the fixed data industry that expressed a view
         considered that satellite services used for fixed data, aviation, maritime and
         government applications are only substitutable to a limited extent or not

         substitutable at all, based on product characteristics, prices and intended use. (228)
         Respondents who consider them to be not substitutable at all pointed out different
         requirements by end users between industry segments. (229)
(179)    From a supply-side perspective, more suppliers in the fixed data industry having
         expressed a view submitted that they cannot switch easily from the supply of
         satellite services for other industries/end-uses to supply satellite services for the
         fixed data industry, in a short time and without incurring significant costs. (230)
         Different requirements and market characteristics would lead to significant cost
         according to these respondents. On the other hand, from the suppliers who
         consider that satellite services are substitutable across different industry segments
         one expressed the view that it does not incur significant costs from switching. (231)
(180)    Regarding the potential further segmentation of the market for the supply of
         satellite services in the fixed data industry between different applications
         (backhaul & trunking, broadband access and enterprise data), more respondents
         having expressed a view considered that the different applications within the fixed
         data industry are interchangeable. (232) One of these respondents referred to the
         different applications as mere ‘marketing’ of the same product while another
         noted that substitutability depends on a case-by-case basis. (233) Respondents who
         do not consider that the different applications are fungible within the fixed data
         industry submitted that there are different requirements for power, product
         characteristics and technologies between the applications. Moreover, they argued
         that backhaul and trunking terminals and baseband usually need to be replaced
         and that higher service level agreements are required for enterprise networks
         compared to broadband networks. (234)
(181)    From a supply side perspective, suppliers in the fixed data industry having
         expressed a view are split as to whether they can switch easily from supplying
         satellite services for a particular application (e.g., backhaul & trunking) to
         supplying satellite services for a different application (e.g., enterprise data) in a
         short time and without incurring significant costs. (235) Respondents who do not
         consider that switching is easy and quick stated that each use case is different, and
         that considerable cost and time would be needed for implementing a switch. (236)
         One of the suppliers who holds the view that suppliers are able to switch between
         different applications within the fixed data industry submitted that the
         applications are interchangeable but still need extra ground equipment. (237)
(182)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that, for the purposes of this
         Decision, there is likely a separate market for the supply of satellite services to
         customers in the fixed data industry. However, for the purposes of the Decision,
         the exact product market definition can be left open, including the question
         whether this potential market should be further segmented by applications
         (backhaul & trunking, broadband access and enterprise data), because the

5.3.2.3.5.      Conclusion on the relevant product market for the supply of satellite
                services
(183)     The Commission considers that, for the purposes of this Decision, it can be left
          open whether the supply of satellite services in each of the aviation, maritime,
          government and fixed data industries, and any potential segments thereof,
          constitute distinct product markets, as the Transaction does not raise serious
          doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market irrespective of whether a
          broader market comprising all industry segments, or separate markets for each of
          the aviation, maritime, government and fixed data industries are defined.
(184)     On a conservative basis, in this Decision the Commission carries out an
          assessment separately in relation to each of the aviation, maritime, government
          and fixed data industries, and any potential segments thereof.

5.3.3.    Geographic market definition

5.3.3.1. Commission’s precedents
(185)     In Astrium Holding/Vizada Group and Apax Partners/Telenor Satellite Services,
          the Commission considered that the market for the supply of satellite connectivity
          services at ‘wholesale level’, i.e., by SSPs to downstream customers (large end
          customers and resellers) is worldwide in scope. (238) In Viasat/Inmersat the
          Commission considered that the market for the supply of IFC services to business
          aviation customers is worldwide in scope, while leaving the question open
          whether the market for the supply of broadband IFC services to commercial
          aviation customers is EEA-wide or worldwide. (239)

5.3.3.2. Notifying Party’s views
(186)     The Notifying Party submits that the appropriate geographic scope for the supply
          of satellite services in the aviation, maritime, government and fixed data
          industries is at least Europe-wide or global in scope, for the following reasons.
(187)     First, competitors supplying satellite services in one or more of the relevant
          industries face a common set of rival suppliers, many of whom have established
          market presence within multiple industry segments on a global or near-global
          basis. As such there is a diverse range of well-connected, global competitors
          providing satellite services across all industries worldwide. (240)
(188)     Second, in Europe, prices across industries are typically subject to external
          pressures that are largely consistent across the region, particularly under current
          market conditions where an influx of new LEO alternatives are now available to
          satellite services customers. (241)
(189)     Third, within Europe, there is a commonality in consumption preferences
          amongst customers within the same industry regardless of their geographic


(238)    Commission decisions of 20 August 2007 in case M.4709 - Apax Partners/Telenor Satellite
         Services, para. 18; and of 30 November 2011 in case M.6393 - Astrium Holding/Vizada Group,
         para.32.
(239)    Commission decision of 25 May 2023 in case M.10807 - Viasat/Inmarsat, recitals 134, 153.
(240)    Form CO, paras 616, 619-20.
(241)    Form CO, para. 617.
                                                41
         location. For example, with respect to the industries for which the Parties overlap
         in the supply of satellite services:
         (a)    In the fixed data industry, the overall needs of a mobile network operator
                customer (i.e., a customer within the fixed data industry) are largely similar
                across the Member States. Whilst requirements for volumetric and
                bandwidth specifications may differ, the core infrastructure and know-how
                required to service a customer are in many cases standardised. Furthermore,
                the Parties’ most significant fixed data customers operate in multiple
                countries and therefore acquire satellite services across multiple countries.
         (b)    In the government sector, the supply of satellite services tends to take place
                on a global basis owing to the dispersed connection needs of the customers.
                Governments may procure, for example, satellite services from suppliers in
                order to securely connect embassies or defence forces around the
                world. (242)

5.3.3.3. Commission’s assessment

5.3.3.3.1.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation industry
(190)    The market investigation indicated that the geographic scope of the market for the
         supply of satellite services in the aviation industry is worldwide.
(191)    The vast majority of respondents having expressed a view submitted that the
         conditions of competition for the supply of satellite services in the aviation
         industry (e.g., prices, customers’ preferences, number and identity of suppliers,
         their market strength) are largely similar/homogenous at the worldwide level. (243)
(192)    Furthermore, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view confirmed
         that they negotiate contracts for the supply of satellite services in the aviation
         industry at the worldwide level. (244)

5.3.3.3.2.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry
(193)    The market investigation indicated that the geographic scope of the market for the
         supply of satellite services in the maritime industry is worldwide.
(194)    All respondents having expressed a view submitted that the conditions of
         competition for the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry (e.g.,
         prices, customers’ preferences, number and identity of suppliers, their market
         strength) are largely similar/homogenous at the worldwide level. (245)
(195)    Furthermore, all respondents having expressed a view confirmed that they
         negotiate contracts for the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry at
         the worldwide level. (246)

5.3.3.3.3.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the government sector
(196)    The market investigation indicated that the geographic scope of the market for the
         supply of satellite services in the government sector may be worldwide or EEA-
         wide.

(197)    More respondents having expressed a view indicated that the conditions of
         competition for the supply of satellite services in the government sector (e.g.,
         prices, customers’ preferences, number and identity of suppliers, their market
         strength) are largely similar/homogenous at the worldwide or the EEA-wide
         level. (247) Factors considered most important were the identity of providers of
         satellite services and the technical reach of satellites. (248)
(198)    Furthermore, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view confirmed
         that they negotiate contracts for the supply of satellite services in the government
         sector at the worldwide level, with EEA-wide following as second-most frequent
         response. (249)

5.3.3.3.4.      Market for the supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry
(199)    The market investigation indicated that the geographic scope of the market for the
         supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry is regional.
(200)    The majority of respondents having expressed a view submitted that the
         conditions of competition for the supply of satellite services in the fixed data
         industry (e.g., prices, customers’ preferences, number and identity of suppliers,
         their market strength) are largely similar/homogenous at regional level. (250) In
         particular, some respondents indicated that Europe – as opposed to other broad
         regions such as Africa and Middle East – could constitute a relevant region in
         which the conditions of competition are largely homogenous, while some other
         respondents pointed to narrower regions within the EEA, such as Western
         Europe. (251)
(201)    At the same time, the majority of respondents having expressed a view confirmed
         that they negotiate contracts for the supply of satellite services in the fixed data
         industry at the worldwide level. (252)

5.3.3.3.5.      Conclusion on the relevant geographic market for the supply of satellite
                services
(202)    The Commission considers that, for the purposes of this Decision, the precise
         geographic scope of the market can be left open irrespective of whether a broader
         market comprising all industry segments, or separate markets for each of the
         aviation, maritime, government and fixed data industries are defined, as the
         Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal
         market irrespective of the exact geographic market definition.
(203)    In this Decision the Commission carries out its assessment on the basis of both
         global and EEA-wide markets in relation to the supply of satellite services in the
         aviation, maritime, government and fixed data industries. In addition, the
         Commission’s competitive assessment regarding the supply of satellite service in
         the fixed data industry also considers potential regional markets within the EEA,
         namely Western Europe (mentioned by certain respondents, as indicated in
         paragraph (200)) as well as other regions such as Eastern Europe, CEE, and

6.       COMPETITIVE ASSESSMENT

6.1.     Introduction
(204)    Section 6.2 sets out the legal framework, and Section 6.3 lays out the market
         shares of the Parties and their competitors in all the affected markets (253) based
         on the relevant markets set out in Section 5.
(205)    The Transaction gives rise to horizontally affected markets between the Parties’
         activities in: (i) the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes); and (ii) the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity. Section
         6.4 assesses the horizontal relationships between the Parties’ activities in the
         affected markets. (254)
(206)    In addition, the Transaction will lead to a potential horizontal overlap in the
         market for the supply of satellite services used in the commercial aviation
         industry, where Intelsat is currently active and SES had plans to enter prior to and
         irrespective of the Transaction. Section 6.5 assesses this potential horizontal
         relationship between the Parties’ activities.
(207)    Furthermore, the Transaction gives rise to vertically affected markets between the
         Parties’ activities in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity
         (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in each of the commercial aviation,
         maritime, fixed data and government industries (downstream). Section 6.6
         assesses these vertical relationships between the Parties’ activities in the affected
         markets.

6.2.     Legal framework
(208)    Under Article 2(2) and (3) of the Merger Regulation, the Commission must assess
         whether a proposed concentration would significantly impede effective
         competition in the internal market or in a substantial part of it, in particular
         through the creation or strengthening of a dominant position.
(209)    In this respect, a merger may entail horizontal and/or non-horizontal (namely,
         vertical or conglomerate) effects. Horizontal effects are those deriving from a
         concentration where the undertakings concerned are actual or potential
         competitors of each other in one or more of the relevant markets concerned. Non-
         horizontal effects can be vertical or conglomerate. Vertical effects are those
         deriving from a concentration where the undertakings concerned are active on
         different or multiple levels of the supply chain. Conglomerate effects are those

(253)   Affected markets are all relevant product and geographic markets, as well as plausible alternative
        relevant product and geographic markets, where the parties’ activities overlap horizontally or are
        vertically related and which do not meet the conditions for review under point 5(d) of the
        Commission Notice on a simplified treatment of certain concentrations under Council Regulation
        (EC) No 139/2004 on the control of concentrations between undertakings, OJ C 160, 5.5.2023, p. 1
        (‘Notice on Simplified Procedure’), and do not benefit from the flexibility clauses of point 8 of
        the Notice on Simplified Procedure. Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2023/914 of 20
        April 2023 implementing Council Regulation (EC) No 139/2004 on the control of concentrations
        between undertakings, OJ L 119, 5.5.2023, p. 2, Annex I, para. 25(g).
(254)   The Transaction results in additional horizontal overlaps for a number of other potential markets.
        However, all of these overlaps fall under point 5 or 8 of the Notice on Simplified Procedure. As a
        result, they are not considered as affected markets and are therefore not discussed in this Decision.
                                                    44
         deriving from a concentration where the undertakings concerned are in a
         relationship which is neither horizontal nor vertical. A concentration may involve
         more types of effects. In such a case, the Commission will appraise horizontal and
         non-horizontal effects in accordance with the guidance set out in the Horizontal
         Merger Guidelines (255) and the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines. (256)
(210)    In assessing the competitive effects of a merger, the Commission compares the
         competitive conditions that would result from the notified merger with the
         conditions that would have prevailed without the merger. In most cases, the
         competitive conditions existing at the time of the merger constitute the relevant
         comparison for evaluating the effects of a merger. However, in some
         circumstances, the Commission may take into account future changes to the
         market that can reasonably be predicted. (257)
(211)    In relation to horizontal effects, the Horizontal Merger Guidelines (258) describe
         two main ways in which horizontal mergers may significantly impede effective
         competition. In particular, the proposed concentration might be creating or
         strengthening a dominant position: (i) by eliminating important competitive
         constraints on one or more firms, which consequently would have increased
         market power, without resorting to coordinated behaviour (non-coordinated
         effects); and (ii) by changing the nature of competition in such a way that firms
         that previously were not coordinating their behaviour, are significantly more
         likely to coordinate and raise prices or otherwise harm effective competition
         (coordinated effects).
(212)    A merger giving rise to horizontal non-coordinated effects might significantly
         impede effective competition by creating or strengthening the dominant position
         of a single firm, one which, typically, would have an appreciably larger market
         share than the next competitor post-merger. Moreover, also mergers that do not
         lead to the creation of or the strengthening of a single firm’s dominant position
         may give rise to competition concerns under the substantive test set out in Article
         2(2) and Article 2(3) of the Merger Regulation. Regarding mergers in
         oligopolistic markets, the Merger Regulation clarifies that ‘under certain
         circumstances, concentrations involving the elimination of important competitive
         constraints that the merging parties exerted upon each other, as well as a
         reduction of competitive pressure on the remaining competitors, may, even in the
         absence of a likelihood of coordination between the members of the oligopoly,
         result in a significant impediment to effective competition’. (259)
(213)    The Horizontal Merger Guidelines list a number of factors which may influence
         whether or not significant horizontal non-coordinated effects are likely to result
         from a merger, such as the large market shares of the merging firms, the fact that
         the merging firms are close competitors, the limited possibilities for customers to
         switch suppliers, or the fact that the merger would eliminate an important



(255)   Guidelines on the assessment of horizontal mergers under the Council Regulation on the control of
        concentrations between undertakings, OJ C 31, 5.2.2004, p. 5 (‘Horizontal Merger Guidelines’).
(256)   Guidelines on the assessment of non-horizontal mergers under the Council Regulation on the control
        of concentrations between undertakings, OJ C 265, 18.10.2008, p. 6 (‘Non-Horizontal Merger
        Guidelines’).
(257)   Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 20.
(258)   Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 22.
(259)   Merger Regulation, recital 25. Similar wording is also found in para. 25 of the Horizontal Merger
        Guidelines.
                                                   45
          competitive force. Not all those factors need to be present to make significant
          non-coordinated effects likely and it is not an exhaustive list. (260)
(214)     In relation to non-horizontal non-coordinated effects, the Non-Horizontal
          Merger Guidelines distinguish between two broad types of non-horizontal
          relationships: vertical relationships and conglomerate relationships. Vertical
          relationships involve companies operating at different levels of a supply chain
          where the vertical relationship is that of supplier or customer, whereas
          conglomerate relationships are between firms that are in a relationship which is
          neither horizontal nor vertical. In practice, the Commission will focus its
          assessment on mergers between firms that are active in closely related markets
          (e.g. mergers involving suppliers of complementary products or products that
          belong to the same product range). (261)
(215)     According to the Non-horizontal Merger Guidelines, non-coordinated effects may
          significantly impede effective competition as a result of a vertical merger if such
          merger gives rise to foreclosure concerns. Foreclosure occurs where actual or
          potential competitors’ access to supplies or markets is hampered or eliminated as
          a result of the merger, thereby reducing those companies’ ability and/or incentive
          to compete. (262) Such foreclosure may discourage entry or expansion of
          competitors or encourage their exit. (263)
(216)     The Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines distinguishes between two forms of
          vertical foreclosure. Input foreclosure occurs where the merger is likely to raise
          the costs of downstream competitors by restricting their access to an important
          input. (264) Customer foreclosure occurs where the merger is likely to foreclose
          upstream competitors by restricting their access to a sufficient customer base. (265)
(217)     Foreclosure may also take more subtle forms, such as the degradation of the
          quality of input supplied. In its assessment, the Commission may consider a series
          of alternative or complementary possible strategies. (266)

6.2.1.    Input foreclosure
(218)     Pursuant to the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, input foreclosure arises
          where, post-merger, the new entity would be likely to restrict access to the
          products or services that it would have otherwise supplied absent the merger,
          thereby raising its downstream rivals’ costs by making it harder for them to obtain
          supplies of the input under similar prices and conditions as absent the merger. (267)
(219)     For input foreclosure to be a concern, the vertically integrated firm should have a
          significant degree of market power in the upstream market. Only when the merged
          firm has such a significant degree of market power, can it be expected that it will
          significantly influence the conditions of competition in the upstream market and
          thus, possibly, the prices and supply conditions in the downstream market. (268)


(260)    Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 26.
(261)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paras 3-5.
(262)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 18.
(263)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 29.
(264)    The term ‘input’ is used here as a generic term and may also cover services. Non-Horizontal Merger
         Guidelines, footnote 24.
(265)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 30.
(266)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 33.
(267)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 31.
(268)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, para. 35.
                                                    46
(220)     In assessing the likelihood of an anticompetitive input foreclosure scenario, the
          Commission examines, first, whether the merged entity would have, post-merger,
          the ability to substantially foreclose access to inputs, second, whether it would
          have the incentive to do so, and third, whether a foreclosure strategy would have
          a significant detrimental effect on competition downstream. (269)
(221)     The three conditions for assessing vertical effects (ability, incentive, effect) are
          cumulative. The absence of any of them is sufficient to rule out the likelihood of
          anti-competitive input foreclosure. (270)

6.2.2.    Customer foreclosure
(222)     Pursuant to the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, customer foreclosure may
          occur when a supplier integrates with an important customer in the downstream
          market and because of this downstream presence, the merged entity may foreclose
          access to a sufficient customer base to its actual or potential rivals in the upstream
          market (the input market) and reduce their ability or incentive to compete, which
          in turn, may raise downstream rivals’ costs by making it harder for them to obtain
          supplies of the input under similar prices and conditions as absent the merger.
          This may allow the merged entity profitably to establish higher prices on the
          downstream market. (271)
(223)     For customer foreclosure to be a concern, a vertical merger must involve a
          company which is an important customer with a significant degree of market
          power in the downstream market. If, on the contrary, there is a sufficiently large
          customer base, at present or in the future, that is likely to turn to independent
          suppliers, the Commission is unlikely to raise competition concerns on that
          ground. (272)
(224)     In assessing the likelihood of an anticompetitive customer foreclosure scenario,
          the Commission examines, first, whether the merged entity would have the ability
          to foreclose access to downstream markets by reducing its purchases from its
          upstream rivals, second, whether it would have the incentive to reduce its
          purchases upstream, and third, whether a foreclosure strategy would have a
          significant detrimental effect on consumers in the downstream market. (273)
(225)     The three conditions for assessing vertical effects (ability, incentive, effect) are
          cumulative. The absence of any of them is sufficient to rule out the likelihood of
          anti-competitive customer foreclosure. (274)

(284)   Form CO, para. 858 and Annexes 16 and 32. Transmission-based market shares for all the tables in
        section 6.3.1 have been extracted from Lygnsat’s dataset for 2023 and 2024 (‘Lyngsat dataset’).
        Lyngsat consolidates information on TV and radio channels transmitted via satellites. Satellite
        operators including the Parties subscribe to Lyngsat to receive quarterly data, including on media
        satellite transmissions, covering elements such as channel name, satellite, frequency band, beam.
(285)   Form CO, para. 891 and Annex 32. Revenue-based market shares for all the tables in section 6.3.1

(286)   Form CO, paras 857-58. With respect of the appropriate methodology for calculation of market
        shares for the supply of one-way satellite capacity in the media segment, the Parties consider
        transmission-based shares to be appropriate as this is the method used by both Parties in the ordinary
        course of business to estimate their respective market positions. The Parties do not consider
        revenues to be appropriate for the assessment of the Transaction because: (i) due to data limitations,
        the Parties are unable to estimate revenue-based shares for their competitors; and (ii) the Parties’
        own revenue figures are based on billing country for the customer, not on the location where satellite
        capacity is supplied. Thus, all revenues relating to a customer located in a European country that
        also obtains satellite capacity for transmissions in other jurisdictions would be allocated to that
        European country (even though that satellite capacity is not provided in Europe). The Parties’
        revenues for certain European countries are therefore likely outsized as compared to their actual
        competitive presence in those countries. Therefore, in turn the revenue-based shares likely
        overestimate the Parties’ respective competitive presence. The Parties note that the transmissions-
        based market shares estimates are not affected by a similar issue.

         (289)   Form CO, para. 864. The Notifying Party submits that the identification of transmissions to Western
                 Europe and Eastern Europe is not part of the Lyngsat data, but it is added by SES by manually
                 matching the information on the satellite used for each transmission with information on the
                 geographic area covered by the satellite beam, as extracted from SatBeams Data. As noted in
                 footnote 287, it is based on a definition of Western Europe and Eastern Europe, and it excludes the
                 regions ‘Russia & CIS’ and ‘Middle-East & North Africa’.
         (290)   Table included because the same market is an affected market for transmissions.
         (291)   Revenues for Eastern Europe are identified based on where the service is being provided.
         (292)   Notifying Party’s response of 5 May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 9, para. 1.8. The Parties note that
                 the definition of Western Europe and Eastern Europe adopted by Analysys Mason (and followed to
                 identify the relevant Parties’ media revenues to be used as numerators) differs from the definition
                 adopted by SES to identify the relevant target region of the channel transmissions listed in the
                 Lyngsat dataset. Most notably, Analysys Mason’s definition of Eastern Europe includes countries
                 such as Russia and Turkey, which have a large demand for DTH and FTA channel transmissions.
                 As such, these revenue-based market shares estimates are not directly comparable with the
                 transmissions-based market shares.

      (297)   Notifying Party’s response of 14 May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 10, paras 5.1-5.2. For Eastern and
              Western Europe, the Notifying Party has only provided revenue-based market shares for FTA and
              cable distribution combined. The Notifying Party submits that the Parties are not aware of any third-
              party report that estimates separately the revenues generated in the FTA and cable distributions two
              segments. In particular, revenue market sizes disaggregating between the FTA and cable
              distribution segments are not available in the Analysys Mason reports. In other cases, the Parties
              used the available data on the number of channel transmissions recorded in the Lyngsat dataset to
              approximate the relative size of the FTA and Cable Feed markets in Europe and globally. The
              Parties consider this to be an imperfect proxy even when estimating the size of larger geographical
              markets. However, the hypothetical markets for FTA and cable distribution in Western and Eastern
              Europe are very small and thus the Parties do not consider that revenue-based shares calculated
              using this proxy would be meaningful. The Parties note that Intelsat generates […] revenues in both
              of these segments in both Western and Eastern Europe (in particular, […]).
