          6.4.1.1. Notifying Party’s views
          (251)      As mentioned above in Section 5.2.2.2, the Notifying Party submits that the
                     supply of satellite capacity should be considered as a whole, with no sub-
                     segmentations, and the Transaction would therefore have no detrimental
                     competitive impact on the market for supply of satellite capacity. (311)
          (252)      However, even if a narrow market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity is
                     considered, the Notifying Party also considers that Transaction has no detrimental
                     impact in competition in the EEA (312) for the reasons set out below.
          (253)      First, the Notifying Party submits that there are several alternative suppliers of
                     satellite capacity for media purposes (including when considering separately
                     DTH, FTA and cable feed distribution), both globally and in Europe, including
                     the following: Eutelsat, At&T, Echostar Hughes, Telesat, INSAT, StarOne,

          (309)     See clarifications in paragraph (230).
          (310)     The Commission assumes the figures provided by the Parties for the ‘Combined share’ to be a sum
                    rounded to the nearest decimal place.
          (311)     Form CO, paras 626, 776-854 and 903.
          (312)     Form CO, para. 903.
                                                              77
         RSCC, Hispasat, Space Norway and ArabSat/Hellas Sat. In Europe in particular:
         (i) Eutelsat, Space Norway and ArabSat/Hellas Sat would be all larger in the
         media segment than Intelsat; and (ii) Eutelsat would be the leading competitor in
         the market with a share of [30-40]%. (313)
(254)    Second, the Notifying Party submits that Intelsat is not a significant competitor in
         the supply of satellite capacity in the media segment in Europe. The increment
         arising from the Transaction is less than 10%: Intelsat’s market share is only [5-
         10]% in the overall market, [5-10]% in DTH distribution, [5-10]% in cable
         distribution and [0-5]% in FTA distribution. Further, Intelsat would be only the
         […] largest competitor (in the overall market for the supply of satellite capacity
         in the media segment and any sub-segments thereof) whereas Eutelsat in
         particular would hold a number of premium orbital locations for servicing
         European customers and would have much greater competitive presence across
         Europe. (314)
(255)    Third, the Notifying Party considers that the Parties are not close competitors in
         the supply of satellite capacity in the media segment in Europe. SES views
         Eutelsat as its most significant competitor in Europe because:
         (i) like SES, Eutelsat supplies satellite capacity (for DTH, FTA and cable
         distribution) across Europe but is largely focused on Western Europe countries
         like Germany and the UK; and (ii) SES and Eutelsat supply satellite capacity for
         media distribution from the same orbital location (the Astra constellation at 28.2°
         East) which means that the cost of switching would be reduced.
(256)    The Notifying Party considers that Intelsat, on the other hand, is close to Space
         Norway because: (i) Intelsat is active primarily in DTH in only a small number of
         countries in Central and Eastern Europe, namely Slovakia, Czech Republic,
         Romania, Hungary and Bulgaria; and (ii) Intelsat supplies satellite capacity for
         DTH in Slovakia, Czech Republic, Romania and Hungary via Space Norway’s
         Thor 5, 6 and 7 satellites as 1° West and Intelsat’s 10-02 satellites at 1° West. As
         such, it is relatively easy for customers to switch between Intelsat and Space
         Norway, given that the transponders are located on the same satellite and no
         adjustment of antennas would be required on consumer households. (315)
(257)    Fourth, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties both share orbital locations
         with third party satellite operators in Europe. The Parties have the right to
         commercialize capacity on satellites that are owned by third parties. For example,
         in Slovakia, Czech Republic, Romania and Hungary, Intelsat is active via Space
         Norway’s Thor 5, 6 and 7 satellites as 1° West. Conversely, a third party
         (Eutelsat) has a right to commercialise capacity on a SES satellite (SES Astra
         satellites located at 28.5° East and 28.2° East orbital location). (316)
(258)    Fifth, the Notifying Party submits that satellite usage in the media segment is
         constrained by the widespread adoption of alternative distribution methods (IP-
         based terrestrial distribution as described in paragraph (30)). (317) According to
         the Notifying Party, the same trend of increased adoption would regard also the



(313)   Form CO, paras 904-22.
(314)   Form CO, paras 919-22.
(315)   Form CO, paras 932-54.
(316)   Form CO, paras 923-31.
(317)   Notifying Party’s White Paper on Media, 25 March and 5 May 2025, in Annex 2 to Notifying
        Party’s response of 5 May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 9.
                                               78
         IPTV (paragraph (34)). (318) The Notifying Party refers, first, to its media
         customers (i.e., broadcasters) heavily investing in OTT and Internet-based
         streaming services to deliver their content directly to audiences, rather than via
         satellite operators. (319) In addition, the Notifying Party refers to consumer trends
         demonstrating a clear preference for streaming, i.e., ‘cutting the cord’, as shown
         by a third-party report (320) showing for example that the global satellite pay-DTH
         market has shed 35 million subscribers over the past five years. (321)
(259)    According to the Notifying Party, there would be little to no marginal
         costs involved for a pay-TV subscriber to switch from a satellite to IP-based
         terrestrial distribution: most viewers already have an internet connection in place
         that would enable them to access the content, and broadcasters would only need
         to ensure the necessary content infrastructure is in place (e.g. by feeding content
         into the terrestrial stream or, for streaming-based services, maintaining the
         required software or app infrastructure). The vast majority of broadcasters or
         content providers would have already such infrastructure in place and cloud
         companies would also be offering cloud-based distribution solutions to media
         companies. Further, the Internet-based OTT distribution model would allow for
         more targeted advertising than would be possible over satellite due to the data that
         broadcasters can obtain on viewing habits. (322)
(260)    According to the Notifying Party, IP-based terrestrial distribution includes fibre,
         as well as fixed Very High-Capacity Network (‘VHCN’) connections (e.g., DSL)
         and mobile connectivity from 5G networks, all of which would be cheap and
         reliable alternatives to satellite distribution and readily available to almost
         everyone throughout Europe. (323) According to the Notifying Party, a full fibre
         penetration would not be required, and the relevant metric would be the
         availability of a sufficiently fast internet connection to download and/or view
         content irrespective of the technology that supports this. As concerns scarcely
         populated or rural areas: internet connection could be achieved through LEO


(318)   Notifying Party’s White Paper on Media, 25 March and 5 May 2025, in Annex 2 to Notifying
        Party’s response of 5 May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 9, paras 2.14, 2.16, 2.25, 2.30, 2.33, 2.34,
        2.36.
(319)   Form CO, paras 979, 982. The Notifying Party submits that broadcasters are taking content that was
        previously distributed exclusively over cable or broadcast and distributing it OTT. The Notifying
        Party refers to Novaspace, ‘Satellite Connectivity and Video Market’, 31 st edition, September 2024,
        in Form CO Annex 32, which indicates that in the coming decade ‘[a] growing number of
        broadcasters are expected to end satellite distribution to solely focus on OTT distribution’. The
        Notifying Party offers examples of media customers migrating from satellite to terrestrial
        alternatives: in […], Intelsat’s customer […] is migrating to IPTV and reducing the amount of
        satellite capacity obtained from Intelsat (Form CO, paras 949-50, 1005-06). See also Intelsat
        Confidential Annexes 43 and 44 to the Form CO. The same Intelsat Confidential Annexes evidence
        that there is ‘pressure’ from […] ‘to terminate DTH’ in the […]); in […] dropped its DTH service
        in January 2025 and it only offers IPTV to its customers (Form CO, para. 1007).
(320)   Novaspace, ‘Satellite Connectivity and Video Market’, 31st edition, September 2024, Chapter 2.1
        ‘Video Distribution and Contribution Services’, page 65 (in Form CO Annex 32), indicates that in
        2023 specifically, pay-DTH subscriptions declined by 3.6% year-over-year, driven by stronger free-
        to-air competition and a growing number of homes cutting the cord and moving to OTT services.
        Furthermore, pay-DTH subscriptions are expected to lose an additional 38 million subscribers
        globally over the next ten years. In the meantime, there were 154 active pay‐DTH platforms
        worldwide as of year-end 2023, four fewer than in 2022 (with a decrease of 2.5%) and 14 fewer
        than five years ago (CAGR of -1.7%). The total number of active pay‐DTH platforms is forecast to
        drop by over 30 units by 2033 (CAGR of -2.1%).
(321)   Form CO, paras 967-78, 1037.
(322)   Form CO, paras 979-86.
(323)   Notifying Party’s response of 5 May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 9, question 8.
                                                    79
         satellite connectivity, 5G, fixed VHCN, and fibre; (324) and individual subscribers
         (who may have access to fewer options in terms of Internet connectivity) are not
         the customers of the Parties (who are the broadcasters and platform operators, for
         which a single price is negotiated for the entire satellite reach). (325)
(261)    Sixth, the Notifying Party submits that market shares tend to reflect past
         competition at the point a media platform was launched and are not reflective of
         significant current or future competitive constraints. The primary point at which
         competition takes place is when a new media platform is launched, which has
         become increasingly rare. Once a broadcaster has acquired viewers with antennas
         pointed at specific satellites, switching between satellite operators is rare and
         limited to exceptional cases where satellite operators offer capacity on the same
         satellite, or following merger activity where the vertical integration may outweigh
         the switching costs involved. As such, instances of switching are rare, as are
         instances of new ‘greenfield’ opportunities (i.e. entirely new media customers,
         platforms and broadcasters entering the European market). Therefore, the Parties
         consider that competitive pressure instead comes primarily from IP-based
         terrestrial distribution. The main engagements between satellite operators and
         media customers typically arise in the context of a contract renewal and the
         Parties’ internal documents would demonstrate that they have lost customers to
         IP-based terrestrial distribution. In particular, as noted above the Notifying Party
         submits that two major alternatives to satellite distribution are fibre and OTT
         distribution. (326)
(262)    Seventh, the Notifying Party submits that, as a result of the demand-side uptake
         of streaming and supply-side uptake of terrestrial, the number of linear TV
         channels being distributed is decreasing. This is supported by a Novaspace report
         which indicates that the number of TV channels has decreased for three of the
         past five years (327), particularly in Western Europe (and North America). (328)
(263)    Eighth, the Notifying Party further submits that another consequence of this trend
         is that satellite revenues in the media segment are decreasing. This is supported
         by the Novaspace 2024 report which indicates that the revenue of satellite
         operators from global video services fell by over USD 20 billion in the past five
         years to USD 84 billion in 2023. The same report anticipates another USD 20
         billion decrease in global video revenues to USD 63.4 billion by 2033. SES’s own
         revenues in the media segment have decreased by 30.1% between 2017 and 2023
         and Intelsat’s media revenues have decreased by 29.7% across the same time
         period. (329)

        connectivity for all by 2030’.

(264)    Ninth, the Notifying Party considers that media customers would have significant
         countervailing buyer power. Customers would generally be sophisticated, large-
         scale businesses such as DTH platform providers, content providers, commercial
         and state broadcasters, popular programming channels and sports rights holders.
         Media customers are also among the Parties’ most significant customer groups in
         terms of revenues (in 2023, the supply of satellite capacity to such customers
         represented […]% of SES’s satellite capacity revenues and […]% of Intelsat’s
         satellite capacity revenues). When negotiating contracts with the Parties, these
         customers would therefore be able to influence factors such as volume
         commitments and contract length, as well as to refer to the possibility of
         accelerating their conversion to streaming as an alternative to satellite
         distribution. (330)
(265)    Tenth, the Notifying Party submits that contract durations for the supply of one-
         way satellite capacity are decreasing. As customers face increasing competition
         from IPTV and OTT platform providers, the unpredictability of a number of
         commercial factors such as subscriber numbers (for satellite distribution) is
         causing customers to be more risk-averse and therefore require lower volumes of
         satellite capacity for shorter committed periods. A typical duration of a contract
         entered into in recent years would be […] years (and in some cases only […]
         years) in contrast with […] years prior to 2020. (331)
(266)    Last, the Notifying Party submits that, to the extent satellite technologies continue
         to be relevant in media distribution to support internet-based streaming, the
         Parties will face strong competition from LEOs such as Starlink or OneWeb. (332)
         According to the Notifying Party, the Parties face competition from LEO
         competitors in media contribution (e.g., live news or sports coverage; which,
         unlike media distribution, is a point-to-point connection service like fixed data
         and mobility services). (333) (334)

6.4.1.2. Commission’s assessment
(267)    The Commission considers that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as
         to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal effects in the
         market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) and any
         potential segment thereof,at worldwide, EEA-wide or regional (Western or
         Eastern Europe) level.
(268)    This is in particular because, as set out in further detail below, (i) the increment
         accounted for by SES or Intelsat is not significant (section 6.4.1.2.1); (ii) a
         sufficient number of credible alternatives will remain post-Transaction (section
         6.4.1.2.2), (iii) SES is close competitor with Eutelsat (section 6.4.1.2.3); (iv)
         customers (broadcasters) have bargaining power (section 6.4.1.2.4); (v) the
         market is constrained by IP-based terrestrial distribution (section 6.4.1.2.5); (vi)

        decreased to 28.8% in 2023 and it is estimated that in 2029, satellite DTH will represent only 21.5%
        of total pay-TV revenues and 13.6% of all pay-TV subscribers.

(331)   Form CO, paras 961-966.
(332)   Form CO, paras 1042-1044.
(333)   Form CO, paras 1045-1068.

        e.g., live news or sports coverage. The present Decision does not focus on media contribution
        because the Parties’ combined market shares in the EEA remain well below 10% and the increment
        is de minimis (with Intelsat’s share at only [0-5]%). Form CO, paras 1059-60; Notifying Party’s
        response of 5 May 2025 to Commission’s RFI 9, question 4, para. 4.3.

         there is sufficient spare capacity (section 6.4.1.2.6); (vii) the market size is
         declining (section 6.4.1.2.7); and (viii) market respondents consider that the
         Transaction will have a neutral impact on their company and the market (section
         6.4.1.2.8).

6.4.1.2.1.      The increment accounted for by SES or Intelsat is not significant in most
                markets
(269)    Based on the market share data provided by the Notifying Party (Section 6.3.1
         and Table 50), the combined market shares range from c. 20% to c. 40% when
         considering transmissions, and are higher when considering revenues. (335)
         However, the increment accounted for by SES or Intelsat is rather low, sometime
         even minimal, modest or limited depending on the affected market under
         assessment.
(270)    Intelsat’s offering in the following affected markets will only increase SES’s
         position to a limited extent, as indicated below:
         (a)    Increase in the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for
                media purposes)
                •   EEA-wide: [0-5]% (based on transmissions) or [0-5]% (based on
                    revenues).
                •   Eastern Europe: [5-10]% (based on transmissions).
                •   Western Europe: [0-5]% (based on revenues).
         (b)    Increase in DTH distribution
                •   EEA-wide: [5-10]% ([…]).
                •   Eastern Europe: [10-20]% (based on transmissions).
         (c)    Increase in FTA & cable distribution
                •   EEA-wide: [0-5]%. (based on revenues).
                •   Western Europe: [0-5]%. (based on revenues).
         (d)    Increase in FTA distribution
                •   EEA-wide: [0-5]%.
(271)    Further, in the following affected markets, the increment accounted for by Intelsat
         is higher but still moderate:
         (a)    Increase in Market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media
                purposes)
                •   Worldwide: [10-20]% (based on revenues).
         (b)    Increase in DTH distribution
                •   Worldwide: [10-20]% (based on revenues).
         (c)    Increase in FTA & cable distribution
                •   Worldwide: [10-20]% (based on transmissions) or [10-20]% (based on
                    revenues).

(272)    Similarly, in the only remaining affected market, the increment is accounted for
         by SES and it is also moderate:
         (a)     Cable distribution
                 •   Worldwide: [10-20]% (based on transmissions) or [10-20]% (based on
                     revenue).
(273)    Therefore, the Commission concludes that the increment brought about by the
         Transaction is not significant in most markets.

6.4.1.2.2.       A sufficient number of credible competitors remain post-Transaction
(274)    The merged entity will continue to compete with several other suppliers of
         satellite capacity in the market for the supply one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes) and any product segments thereof at a worldwide, EEA-wide or
         regional (Western or Eastern Europe) level.

6.4.1.2.2.1.     Markets affected at worldwide level (336)
(275)    During the market investigation, the majority of market respondents having
         expressed a view considered that there are sufficient alternative suppliers to SES
         and Intelsat in the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes) at worldwide level. (337) Most respondents (338) having expressed a
         view indicated that Eutelsat, ArabSat/Hellas Sat and Telesat are alternative
         suppliers at worldwide level. (339)
(276)    In the potential segment for DTH distribution, most respondents having expressed
         a view considered that both Eutelsat and ArabSat/Hellas Sat are alternative
         suppliers. (340)
(277)    In the potential segments for either FTA or cable distribution, most respondents
         having expressed a view indicated Eutelsat as an alternative at worldwide level.
         In addition, several respondents also indicated Telesat, ArabSat/Hellas Sat,
         Hispasat, Measat, Turksat, Nilesat, AsiaSat and/or ABS as alternatives in the
         potential segment for FTA distribution. Further, several respondents indicated
         AsiaSat and ABS as alternatives in the potential segment for cable distribution.
         (341)
(278)    Given that respondents considered that there are alternative suppliers in the
         narrowest potential segments of FTA or cable distribution when considered
         separately, the Commission considers that the same is applicable to the potential
         wider segment for FTA & Cable (combined).




(336)   To recall, these are: (i) the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes);
        (ii) the potential segment for DTH distribution; (iii) the potential segment for FTA & cable
        distribution; and (iv) the potential segment for cable distribution.

(338)   For the purposes of this decision, ‘most respondents’ refers to the most selected option(s) by market
        respondents when a majority of more than 50% has not been reached.
(339)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question D.A.B.4.
(340)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question D.A.B.4.

        indicated that ArabSat/Hellas Sat was and was not an alternative in the potential segment for FTA
        distribution, which is inconclusive.

6.4.1.2.2.2.     Markets affected at EEA level (342)
(279)    The majority of market respondents having expressed a view considered that there
         are sufficient alternative suppliers to SES and Intelsat in the market for the supply
         of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) in the EEA. (343) Most
         respondents having expressed a view indicated that Eutelsat and Hispasat are
         alternative suppliers in the EEA. (344)
(280)    In the potential segments for DTH, FTA or cable distribution, most respondents
         having expressed a view indicated Eutelsat as an alternative in the EEA. In
         addition, several respondents also indicated Space Norway, ArabSat/Hellas Sat,
         BulgariaSat, Hispasat and/or Telesat as alternatives in the potential segment for
         DTH distribution. Further, several respondents indicated Hispasat and Amos
         Spacecom as alternatives in the potential segment for FTA distribution. In
         addition, several respondents indicated Space Norway and Hispasat as
         alternatives in the potential segment for cable distribution. (345)
(281)    Given that respondents considered that there are alternative suppliers in the
         narrowest potential segments of FTA or cable distribution when considered
         separately, the Commission considers that the same is applicable to the potential
         segment for FTA & Cable (combined).

6.4.1.2.2.3.     Markets affected at regional level (346)
(282)    The majority of respondents having expressed a view submitted that there are not
         sufficient alternatives at a regional level in the market for one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes). (347) Explanations by these respondents relate to
         the difficulty in switching providers in the most relevant orbital locations for a
         given region. (348) The difficulty in switching is addressed in the sections on
         customers (broadcasters) having bargaining power (Section 6.4.1.2.4) and the
         market being constrained by IP-based terrestrial distribution (Section 6.4.1.2.5).
(283)    However, in response to another question of the market investigation, the majority
         of respondents having expressed a view submitted that the several suppliers
         indicated above are alternatives in the market for the supply of one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes) and any potential segments thereof (DTH, FTA or
         cable distribution) in the EEA would be the same at a regional
         level. (349) Therefore, the Commission concludes that there would also be
         alternative suppliers at regional level.

(285)    However, taking into consideration the overall results of the market investigation,
         as well as the Parties’ internal documents, the Commission considers that Eutelsat
         is SES’s closest competitor in the market for one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes) and any product segment thereof (i.e. DTH, FTA and cable distribution)
         at worldwide, EEA-wide and regional level.
(286)    As for Intelsat’s closest competitor at worldwide, EEA-wide or regional level, the
         results of the market investigation are inconclusive. The Commission notes that
         in Europe Intelsat’s satellites are in the same orbital location as competitor Space
         Norway (former Telenor) at 1° West (paragraph (40)). In any event, the fact that
         Eutelsat is the closest competitor to SES indicates that the Parties are not close
         competitors.

         Norway and Thaicom. (351)

         supply of one-way satellite capacity and any product segments thereof (i.e. DTH,
         FTA and cable distribution).

         way satellite capacity and any product segments thereof (i.e. DTH, FTA and cable

         performance and coverage) at worldwide level: (1) SES, (2) Eutelsat, (3) AsiaSat,
         (4) Optus, and (5) AsiaSat. (353) In this regard, one customer stated that ‘both
         Intelsat and SES operate a worldwide satellite fleet paced on adjacent orbital

(291)    However, most responses do not substantiate why SES would be the closest
         competitor to Intelsat at worldwide level. The few substantiated responses seem
         to indicate that either SES or Eutelsat would be the closest competitors to Intelsat.

6.4.1.2.3.2.2. DTH distribution
(300)    In the potential segment for DTH distribution, most respondents having expressed

         for DTH customers within Europe’. (361)

         competitor to SES, Eutelsat is a closer competitor to SES in the potential segment

        Projekten – etwa bei multinationalen Medienkunden, Signalzuführungen, Eventdistribution oder
        Backup-Lösungen – überschneiden sich die Leistungen mit denen von Intelsat. Andere Anbieter sind
        entweder stärker auf klassische TV-Ausstrahlung ausgerichtet (z. B. Eutelsat) oder geografisch und
        technologisch begrenzt. Diese stehen nicht in direkter Konkurrenz zu Intelsat, da deren Zielmärkte,
        Kundengruppen und technische Ausrichtungen stark abweichen’.
(359)   Form CO, Annex 48 […].
(360)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question D.A.C.4.
(361)   Replies to Q1 (Media), question D.A.C.4.1.
                                                   87
         (2) Eutelsat, (3) SES, Space Norway, ArabSat/Hellas Sat or Hispasat (4) Space
         Norway or ArabSat/Hellas Sat, (5) AMOS SpaceCom. (362)
(304)    However, most responses do not substantiate why SES would be the closest
         competitor to Intelsat in the potential segment for DTH distribution. One
         customer explained that ‘Intelsat is at least with the two largest sat capacity
         providers SES and Eutelsat in close competition when providing sat services for
         media purposes’. Another customer explained that ‘depending on the coverage
         and the percentage of DTH dishes pointing to the provider’s orbital locations,
         Intelsat is competing more or less mainly with SES and Eutelsat. Regionwise they
         also compete with the other providers mentioned above.’ (363)
(305)    Therefore, the Commission considers that the market investigation results on the
         closest competitor to Intelsat in the potential segment for DTH distribution at EEA
         level show that SES may be the closest competitor to Intelsat.

6.4.1.2.3.2.3. FTA distribution
(306)    In the potential segment for FTA distribution, most respondents having expressed
         a view ranked the following suppliers as the closest competitors to SES (e.g. in
         terms of price, performance and coverage) in the EEA: (1) Eutelsat, (2) Intelsat,
         (3) Intelsat or Hispasat, and (4) RSCC. (364)
(307)    One customer explained that ‘Eutelsat is the main SES competitors in FTA in
         EEA. SES 19.2E position vs Eutelsat Hotbird 13E position’. Another customer
         justifies the closeness between SES and Eutelsat as follows: ‘technically
         comparable infrastructure: Eutelsat operates one of the main orbital locations
         for FTA distribution in Europe with the Hotbird satellite fleet at 13° East. It is
         comparable to SES’ Astra satellites at 19.2° East, both covering large parts of
         Europe and oriented towards a high density of devices (satellite dishes,
         receivers). Target customer group and content: Both providers serve similar
         audiences, including international broadcasters, public service broadcasters and
         special religious or ethnic programmes, which are distributed in Europe without
         encryption (FTA). This content requires a broad technical coverage, offering both
         Eutelsat and SES. Competition in practice: In day-to-day operational practice
         (e.g. tenders, comparisons of offers), SES and Eutelsat regularly face direct
         competition, especially for FTA programmes with a pan-European or regional
         reach. Other suppliers are not real alternatives in the EEA FTA market: […] The
         market structure in the FTA sector is characterised by two dominant suppliers
         (SES and Eutelsat) – regional differences in scope or intensity of competition are
         not discernible’ (translated). (365)

        stehen SES und Eutelsat regelmäßig in direkter Konkurrenz, insbesondere wenn es um FTA-

(308)    Therefore, the Commission concludes that, while Intelsat may be a close
         competitor to SES, Eutelsat is the closest competitor to SES in the potential
         segment for FTA distribution at EEA level.
(309)    In the potential segment for FTA distribution, most respondents having expressed
         a view ranked the following suppliers as the closest competitors to Intelsat (e.g.
         in terms of price, performance and coverage) in the EEA: (1) Eutelsat, (2) SES,
         (3) Hispasat, and (4) RSCC. (366)
(310)    However, most responses do not substantiate why Eutelsat would be the closest
         competitor to Intelsat in the potential segment for FTA distribution. One customer
         indicated that ‘Intelsat has only one FTA position in Europe - 1West together with
         Telenor. Main competitors - Eutelsat Hotbird 13E and SES 19.2E’. (367)
(311)    Another customer explained that Intelsat ‘is barely active in the tradition FTA
         segments in Europe; the focus is on data, and applications with a specific scope
         requirement.’ (translated). (368)
(312)    Therefore, the Commission considers that the market results on the closest
         competitor to Intelsat in the potential segment for FTA distribution at EEA level
         show that, SES may be the closest competitor Intelsat.

6.4.1.2.3.2.4. Cable distribution
(313)    In the potential segment for cable distribution, most respondents having expressed
         a view ranked the following suppliers as the closest competitors to SES (e.g. in
         terms of price, performance and coverage) in the EEA: (1) Eutelsat, (2) Eutelsat
         or Intelsat, (3) Intelsat, Hispasat or Space Norway, (4) Space Norway, and (5)
         ArabSat/Hellas Sat. (369)
(314)    One customer indicated that ‘it all depends on the selection criteria and the
         geostationary position that best meets the need. For example, the 1°W position
         where Intelsat operates with IS 1002 and Space Norway with the THOR satellites
         will be more suitable for Central and Northern Europe, while other positions held
         by SES and EUTELSAT will offer good overall prospects to the West and
         East.’ Another customer explained that it ‘considers Eutelsat to be the main
         competitor of SES in the transmission of signals to cable networks in the EEA.
         Intelsat can also be used as an alternative in specific use cases, but much more
         limited’ (translated). (370)
(315)    Therefore, the Commission concludes that, while Intelsat may be a close
         competitor to SES, Eutelsat is likely the closest competitor to SES in the potential
         segment for cable distribution at EEA level.

(316)    In the potential segment for cable distribution, most respondents having expressed
         a view ranked the following suppliers as the closest competitors to Intelsat (e.g.
         in terms of price, performance and coverage) in the EEA: (1) SES, (2) Eutelsat,
         (3) Hispasat or ArabSat/Hellas Sat, (4) Space Norway. (371)
(317)    However, most respondents do not substantiate why SES would be the closest
         competitor to Intelsat. One customer explained that SES ‘provides an established
         satellite delivery infrastructure for cable operators in the EEA, including high
         technical coverage, service quality and reliability. In cases where Intelsat is used
         as a cable supply provider, SES is the most obvious alternative, both technically
         and economically’ and that Eutelsat ‘also operates important satellite positions
         (mostly 13° East) for signal delivery to cable head stations across Europe.
         Especially for international FTA or pay-TV signals, Eutelsat is a plausible
         alternative to Intelsat’. (372)
(318)    Therefore, the Commission considers that the market results on the closest
         competitor to Intelsat in the potential segment for cable distribution at EEA level
         show that, SES may be the closest competitor Intelsat.

6.4.1.2.3.2.5. FTA & Cable (combined)
(319)    Given that respondents considered that Eutelsat is the closest competitor to SES
         in the narrowest potential segments of FTA or cable distribution, when considered
         separately, the Commission considers that the same is applicable to the potential
         segment for FTA & Cable (combined). Similarly, the results on the closest
         competitor to Intelsat in this segment show that, SES may be the closest
         competitor Intelsat.

6.4.1.2.3.3.    Markets affected at regional level
(320)    The majority of market respondents having expressed a view considered that the
         above-mentioned ranking of closest suppliers to each of SES and Intelsat in the
         market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) and in
         any potential segments thereof (i.e., including the segment for FTA and cable
         distribution combined) (373) in the EEA would be the same at a regional level (e.g.
         Western and Eastern Europe). (374)
(321)    Further, one customer explained that ‘Eutelsat seems to be the closest competitor
         in central Europe as DTH dishes especially in regions neighbouring to GER/AUT
         like France, Italy, Benelux are substantially also pointing to Eutelsat positions as
         well as to SES ones’. (375)
(322)    Therefore, the Commission concludes that, while Intelsat may be a close
         competitor to SES, Eutelsat would be a closer competitor to SES at regional level.
         As for the closest competitor to Intelsat, the results show that SES may be the
         closest competitor Intelsat.

                                                   90
6.4.1.2.4.      Customers (broadcasters) have bargaining power
(323)    Even though the feedback to the market investigation indicates that switching
         suppliers is difficult and LEO satellites are not a competitive constraint,
         customers (which are mainly broadcasters) consider that they have bargaining
         power, both when purchasing satellite capacity for media purposes and when
         negotiating contract renewals.
(324)    The majority of respondents having expressed a view indicated that to switch their
         supplier of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes), taking into account
         the cost, time and feasibility of switching suppliers, is technically complex,
         disruptive to the business, requires a lot of time and is therefore not a realistic
         option. (376) Once customers have adopted a supplier of one-way satellite capacity
         (for media purposes), in practice, they do not switch to different suppliers. (377)
(325)    One customer submitted that ‘switching to another supplier would require many
         households to replace its satellite dish by a larger one (adding additional
         multifeed hardware) or to completely re-align the dish. This is not a realistic
         option, because it would be too costly’. Similarly, another customer submitted
         that ‘in FTA, once an antenna is installed and a decoder is configured, whether it
         is an individual or a professional affiliated operator, changes are always difficult
         to organize. The individual does not want to install a second antenna, and the
         professional who has multiple antennas will be reluctant to make technical
         changes. The situation is different in DTH where, most often, the offer is packaged
         with the initial installation of the antenna included. In any case, the viewer will
         make the effort to adapt their installation to continue benefiting from the services
         they pay for as part of a subscription. But here too, experience shows that DTH
         operators very rarely change orbital locations’. (378)
(326)    In the view of market participants, this bargaining power exists even though LEOs
         are not considered an alternative for one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes). The majority of respondents indicated that, in the context of the
         negotiations for agreements in the market for the supply of one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes) and any potential segments thereof, LEO
         satellites (for example, Starlink or OneWeb) are not currently mentioned as
         alternatives. (379) This is mainly because LEOs do not have the necessary
         technical characteristics for media distribution. For example:
         (a)    One respondent submitted that ‘LEO systems are primarily focused on two-
                way broadband services and are not yet considered technically or
                commercially viable for traditional broadcast distribution’. (380)
         (b)    Another respondent explained in more detail that ‘those satellites are not
                efficient for video distribution (one to many), LEO have some advantages
                of satellite (easy deployment) and specifically LEO are very suitable for
                two-way data transfer for applications such as IoT or just internet access
                (with little latency), but capacity is not one of their main advantages and
                creating an specific content to address the demand every single ground

                installation is not possible when talking about media (huge data rates even

         (c)    A third customer explained that ‘Leo systems are primarily designed for
                bidirectional data communication (e.g. internet, backhaul, enterprise
                connectivity). For classical TV distribution, the following are currently
                missing: Suitable bandwidth configurations for simultaneous broadcasting
                to many receivers; Stable orbitally fixed reception scenarios (unlike GEO
                systems); Technically established reception infrastructure on the customer
                side (e.g. head stations or end-users with fixed antennas). We have never
                referred to LEOs in contractual discussions or tenders, nor are providers
                currently playing them as an alternative’ (translated). (382)
         (d)    Further, one customer submitted that there are ‘fundamental differences in
                technical capabilities, coverage characteristics, and intended use cases that
                make LEO systems unsuitable for traditional one-way broadcast
                applications’.
         (e)    Another customer submitted that ‘LEO systems are primarily designed for
                two-way data services and lack the consistent, fixed coverage and
                infrastructure compatibility required for traditional linear broadcast
                transmission. GEO satellites remain the standard for reliable, point-to-
                multipoint media deliver’.
(327)    Similarly, another respondent submitted that ‘at this time, LEO does not have the
         reliability, bandwidth, business model and capacity to transmit all our TV and
         Radio programs. In addition, this would create a lot of extra cost for the
         households, which are using standard DVB-S2 receivers in their TV sets or Set
         Top Boxes. To supply this legacy households reliably needs many more years of
         one-way DVB distribution’. (383) Notwithstanding the above, the majority of
         respondents having expressed a view submitted that customers are able to obtain
         favourable terms when purchasing one-way satellite capacity (for media
         purposes) indicating that they have ‘medium customer power’ (e.g. negotiating
         and bargaining power). (384)
(328)    When asked whether customers can obtain favourable terms when purchasing
         one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes), for example negotiating and
         bargaining power, the majority of respondents having expressed a view submitted
         that they have ‘medium customer power’. (385) In particular, the majority of
         respondents having expressed a view submitted that, when negotiating the
         extension of an existing agreement for the supply of one-way satellite capacity
         (for media purposes), customers are able to renegotiate the terms and conditions

         of such agreement with their supplier (and not merely extend the initially agreed
         terms and conditions). (386)
(329)    One respondent submitted that ‘we consider customer power to be medium in the
         CEE region, as there is some room for negotiation on pricing and contract terms,
         especially when multiple providers cover the region. However, limited supplier
         options and long-term infrastructure commitments (e.g. dish alignment) reduce
         overall bargaining power compared to more competitive or mature markets’.
         Another respondent submitted that ‘due to the orbital location lock in there is no
         strong power. However, as Satellite loses a bit of relevance compared to
         streaming, the power is now getting better’. (387)
(330)    Finally and importantly, IP-based terrestrial distribution is a competitive
         constraint in the market for one-way satellite capacity and the Parties’ internal
         documents demonstrate that customers use it, in practice, to obtain better
         commercial terms, as explained in more detail below in Section 6.4.1.2.5.

6.4.1.2.5.      The market is constrained by IP-based terrestrial distribution
(331)    The Commission’s market investigation has tested whether terrestrial
         broadcasting technologies (for example, fibre) are currently a credible alternative
         to the Parties, which was confirmed by most of the respondents having expressed
         a view. (388) For example, respondents submitted that:
         (a)    ‘terrestrial telco services and internet access are the strongest competitors
                for satellite broadcast’;
         (b)    ‘fiber optics, private contributions but also OTT via the public Internet can
                compete with the activities of SES and Intelsat for media distribution and
                contribution, especially in Europe where the rate of equipment and Internet
                connectivity allows this’;
         (c)    ‘other transmission channels, especially the Internet, are becoming
                increasingly popular’;
         (d)    ‘terrestrial broadcasting technologies such as fibre are increasingly seen
                as a credible alternative to satellite capacity, particularly for contribution
                feeds and cable distribution’;
         (e)    ‘with the ongoing expansion of broadband, fibre will become a viable
                alternative not only to cable television but also to satellite television’;
         (f)    ‘for broadcast contribution feeds ideally you have both fibre and satellite
                where and when possible. If for budget reasons there is only place for one
                and (reliable) fibre is available, the location is used regularly and the end
                destination(s) are easy to reach by fibre, fibre usually wins’;
         (g)    ‘Other terrestrial broadcasting technologies are becoming viable
                alternatives’. (389)
(332)    As regards in particular IPTV (paragraph (34)), one customer submitted that
         ‘IPTV increasingly substitutes satellite TV’; another customer submitted that
         ‘IPTV increasingly substitutes satellite TV. This is the reason why [the customer]
         has phased out satellite TV products in many countries in Europe’; another

(333)    When asked to provide examples of past instances where customers switched
         from a satellite supplier of one-way satellite capacity to terrestrial broadcasting
         technologies, several respondents offered such examples (see also the last two

         respondents submitted:
         (a)    ‘we are using terrestrial fibre distribution to some cable headends and
                terrestrial DVB [digital video broadcasting] transmission sites that were

         (b)    ‘In 2024 [customer] switched to fibre technology in the French market’;

                via internet connections rather than satellites’ (translated (392));
         (d)    ‘we bypassed this in the United Kingdom when a major affiliated DTH
                operator offered us very high renewal terms. We then decided to distribute
                our service via the Internet to tablets and connected TVs’;
         (e)    ‘a large percentage of UK households now consume TV content over

                or other standards) for contribution purposes’;
         (g)    ‘we are using terrestrial fibre distribution to some cable headends and
                terrestrial DVB transmission sites that were previously delivered via GEO
                satellite distribution. The costs are much lower. We ceased a beam on SES’s
                NSS12 (east hemi) and replaced it with terrestrial distribution’. (393)
(334)    However, certain respondents submitted that satellite is still preferable in certain
         cases, for example for cross-border TV signal (one customer submitted that
         ‘point-to-point transmission could as well be done via fibre, if you want to spread
         an event to several takers, satellite might be the better and more cost-effective
         option’); and on a large scale (one respondent points out that: ‘transmission is
         scalable: One signal for millions of households, regardless of local
         infrastructure’). According to these respondents to the market investigation, the
         IP-based terrestrial alternatives do not cover all cases (one customer submitted
         that such alternatives are ‘not for universal replacement’), such as in particular:

                as [ours]’; ‘[the customer] currently serves 16 million households in

                ensure cable distribution’. (396)

(336)    In addition, the Commission has assessed the availability of a sufficiently fast
         internet connection, as well as the fibre penetration, in each of the Member States
         where Intelsat is active i.e., Czech Republic, Hungary, Romania, Slovakia, and to
         a lesser extent Ireland and Romania: (398)
         (a)    The DESI data (paragraph (32)) on fibre penetration (399) demonstrate that
                fibre penetration is ramping up across the Member States where Intelsat is
                active.
         (b)    According to the DESI 2024 report, the transmission rate needed for video
                streaming (400) is already achieved and far exceeded by “more than 80% of
                households in all study countries except Latvia. [In Czechia] broadband
                service capable of delivering at least 30Mbps download speeds were
                available to more than 98% of households [and] Slovakia registered the
                highest growth with at least 30Mbps coverage expanding by 14.6
                percentage points in the twelve-month period to the end of June 2023” (401)
         (c)    More generally, the report states that ‘[b]y mid-2023, 93.3% of EU
                households had access to at least one fixed broadband service that provided
                actual download speeds of at least 30Mbps, a 2.5 percentage increase since
                mid-2022’. (402)
(337)    While the availability of fast internet and fibre has not reached 100%, faster
         terrestrial internet connections are deployed throughout the EU. The EU Digital
         Decade policy programme has concrete targets and objectives for 2030, guiding
         Europe’s digital transformation. (403) Among these is the aim to make fixed
         internet connections with a speed of at least 1 Gbps available to everyone. The
         Notifying Party also submits that any uncovered internet connection could be

         achieved through LEO satellite connectivity, (404) though the Commission has not

(338)    The Commission takes into account the argument of the Notifying Party that the
         Parties’ customers are not the individual subscribers who may have access to
         fewer options in terms of Internet connectivity, but the broadcasters and platform
         operators, for which a single price is negotiated for the entire satellite reach. (405)
(339)    Therefore, not only is a fixed internet connection with a sufficient speed to use
         OTT services available to the vast majority of households in the EU already, but,
         in line with the trends of previous years, this number will continue to increase
         over the next few years. As the Commission also has to take future market
         developments into account (see paragraph (210)), it finds that IP-based terrestrial
         distribution are already a significant competitive constraint for one-way satellite
         capacity (for media purposes) and will become an even greater constraint in the
         foreseeable future.

6.4.1.2.6.      There is sufficient spare capacity for one-way transmissions in the market
(340)    The market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) is
         characterized by spare capacity, including amongst the Parties’ competitors.
(341)    The majority of market respondents having expressed a view indicated that there
         is spare capacity amongst the competitors of SES and Intelsat to cover customers’
         demand for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes). (406)
(342)    Further, several respondents indicated that, due to the decrease in demand for one-
         way satellite capacity (discussed in more detail in Section 6.4.1.2.7), could lead
         to an additional increase of capacity available for two-way communication in the
         future. One respondent explained that ‘in our experience, due to the development
         of terrestrial networks (fibre and cellular networks) the demand for one-way
         satellite is decreasing rapidly’. Another customer indicated that ‘the satellite
         capacity for one way video is decreasing because migration to other IP based
         technologies occurs (OTT, IPTV, SRT etc) and also the video processing is
         changing (migration to MPEG-4, HVEC etc), so more and more capacity will be
         available in the future’. (407)
(343)    Therefore, the Parties do not have any pivotal capacity to serve demand that
         cannot be served by competitors and would always face competition from rivals
         who have spare capacity to compete for the Parties’ customers.

6.4.1.2.7.      The market size for one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) is
                declining
(344)    The Parties take into account that ‘the media segment has been in decline for some
         time’ in their deal rationale: to increase the quality for the media solutions offered
         by the merged entity to its customers as it will generate a network with an
         expanded geographical footprint across all subsegments. (408)

(345)    In the market investigation, the majority of respondents having expressed a view
         submitted that the market for one-way satellite capacity (for media purposes) is a
         declining market. (409)
(346)    Several respondents submitted that this decline is particularly observed in the
         EEA. One customer indicated that ‘the supply of one-way satellite capacity for
         media purposes is widely considered to be a declining market, particularly in
         mature regions such as the European Economic Area (EEA). This decline is
         driven by structural shifts in media consumption, technological substitution, and
         industry consolidation. There is a constant shift to IP based delivery methods with
         constant decline of the DTH Subscribers especially in the mature markets like the
         EEA’. Similarly, another respondent submitted that ‘the supply of one-way
         satellite capacity for media purposes is generally a declining market, especially
         in the CEE region, due to the growing shift towards IPTV, streaming services,
         and fibre-based distribution. However, satellite broadcasting still plays a role in
         reaching remote areas and supporting specific use cases such us live events’. (410)
(347)    This decline in market size is also supported by third-party reports. According to
         the Novaspace 2024 report, revenue of satellite operators from global video
         services (including satellite distribution and contribution) fell by over USD
         20 billion in the past five years to USD 84 billion in 2023. (411) The same report
         anticipates another USD 20 billion decrease in global video revenues, from USD
         84 billion in 2023 down to USD 63.4 billion by 2033 (CAGR of -2.8%). (412)
(348)    In addition, this is also supported by the Parties’ internal documents. In a
         competitive analysis dated March 2023, […]. (413) […]. (414)

6.4.1.2.8.      Most market respondents submitted that the Transaction will have a neutral
                impact
(349)    The majority of respondents having expressed a view indicated that they expect
         the Transaction to have a neutral impact on the markets or the companies. (415)
         These market participants do not expect a positive or negative impact of the
         Transaction on either the relevant overall markets or their company. There have
         even been some very few market participants that expect a positive impact.
(350)    There have also been a few market participants that expect a negative impact.
         Certain broadcasters expected that the merged entity could reduce the satellite
         capacity available for media broadcast, which would reduce their negotiating
         position as customers (broadcasters) of the merged entity. The Commission
         considers that broadcasters active in the media segment would have plenty of
         options to switch to any of the Parties’ actual and potential competitors (as
         confirmed by the market investigation, see Section 6.4.1.2.2) in the event of a
         price rise or reduction in supply of capacity by the Parties. (416)

(351)    In addition, several competitors complained about the Transaction as they feared
         increased competition from the merged entity. (417) In particular, one competitor
         voiced concerns that the merged entity would need less capacity from competitors
         and be in a stronger bargaining position for the capacity still needed (i.e., SNO
         purchasing satellite capacity from another SNO). The Commission considers that
         satellite capacity being purchased from other satellite operators is limited (418) and
         fills a specific purpose: where one Party (e.g., SES) has contracted with a satellite
         operator, this is because SES could not serve that particular requirement, and it
         would still need to meet that requirement through a third-party satellite operator
         (and therefore, with the exception of purchases from Intelsat, could not self-
         supply to fill this capacity gap post-Transaction). (419) In addition, given the
         amounts of spare capacity (Section 6.4.1.2.6) and active competitors available
         both globally and in the EEA (Section 6.4.1.2.2), rival satellite operators would
         have a multitude of options to choose from in the event that there is insufficient
         or no satellite capacity available from the merged entity.
(352)    Finally, one competitor complained about the possible reduction of number of
         satellites, which could terminate the use of certain orbital locations for media
         purposes, which would in turn disadvantage competitors using the same orbital
         location. (420) As concerns the SES and Eutelsat overlap in the same orbital
         location 28.2°/28.5° East (paragraph (40)), […]. (421) As concerns the Intelsat and
         Space Norway’s overlap in the same orbital location at 1° West (paragraph (40)),
         the launch of Space Norway's Thor 8 satellite expected in 2027 shows Intelsat's
         long-term commitment to 1° West. (422) In any case, repointing antennas to a new
         position is commercially unattractive for DTH providers in light of the legacy
         nature of DTH services and the risk of subscriber churn (which can be substantial
         and estimated to be at least 10% or potentially more) when DTH providers seek
         to repoint antennas, and subscribers prefer to switch to new DTH or alternative
         services (IPTV or cord-cutting for example). (423)

6.4.1.3. Conclusion
(353)     In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
          not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
          of horizontal effects in the market for the supply of one-way satellite capacity (for
          media purposes) and any potential segments thereof at a worldwide, EEA-wide
          or regional (Western or Eastern Europe) level.

6.4.2.1. Notifying Party’s views
(356)     The Notifying Party considers that the Transaction will not give rise to
          competition concerns in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity,
          at the global or EEA-wide level, for the following reasons.
(357)     First, the Notifying Party submits that the combined market shares of the Parties
          in the supply of two-way satellite capacity are generally modest. (425)
(358)     Second, the Notifying Party states that satellite capacity is fungible across
          different satellite types. As such, the Parties’ supply of satellite capacity via wide-
          beam satellites faces significant competitive constraints from satellite capacity via
          HTS satellites. HTS satellites are technologically more efficient and less costly.
          Because of these advantages over wide-beam satellites, there is a long-term shift
          away from wide-beam satellites towards HTS satellites. (426)
(359)     Third, the Notifying Party notes that satellite capacity is also fungible across
          frequency bands and, therefore, there is a high degree of demand-side
          substitutability across the different frequency bands. Most customers can choose
          to procure satellite capacity using either of the C-, Ka- or Ku- bands. Any
          obstacles to customers switching between frequency bands due to demand-side
          factors such as hardware, are currently small and getting smaller. Many customers
          already have the hardware equipment needed to utilize different frequency bands,

(424)    The Transaction also results in horizontal overlaps in relation to a number of other plausible market
         segments. However, none of these overlaps give rise to affected markets within the meaning of
         recital 25(g) Annex I to the Implementing Regulation, as they meet the conditions for review under
         point 5 of the Notice on Simplified Procedure. Therefore, these overlaps will not be assessed in this
         Decision.
(425)    Form CO, paras 1079, 1087 and Annex 50.
(426)    Form CO, paras 829-34.
                                                     100
         but, even for those who do not, the cost of acquiring new hardware is decreasing.
         In addition, the satellite communications industry is in the process of shifting
         towards a ‘frequency- and hardware-agnostic environment’, increasing satellite
         substitutability even further. Customers are increasingly looking towards multi-
         band solutions, underscoring the substitutability of satellite capacity in the
         different frequency bands. (427)
(360)    Fourth, the Notifying Party submits that there are many other suppliers of two-
         way satellite capacity globally and in Europe, including: Eutelsat, ABS Asia
         Broadcast Satellite, ArabSat/Hellas Sat, Turksat, RSCC and Hispasat, amongst
         others. (428)
(361)    Fifth, the Notifying Party states that there exists spare two-way satellite capacity
         among the Parties and their competitors. (429)
(362)    Sixth, the Notifying Party notes that most customers of satellite capacity, and in
         particular SSP customers, are sophisticated businesses able to exert a significant
         degree of countervailing power. (430)

6.4.2.2. Commission’s assessment
(363)    For the reasons set out below and based on the results of the market investigation,
         the Commission considers that the Transaction would not significantly impede
         effective competition as a result of horizontal non-coordinated effects on the
         market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, or any of its potential
         segments, at a global or EEA-wide level.
(364)    First, the Parties’ market position will remain moderate in most segments. In
         particular, the Parties’ combined shares range between [20-30]% and [40-50]%
         in the EEA and between [30-40]% and [40-50]% at the global level, except in the
         narrow segment for two-way satellite capacity supplied through traditional wide-
         beam satellites via the Ka-band at the global level, where the Parties combined
         share is [60-70]%. It is noted that Intelsat does not supply traditional wide-beam
         capacity using the Ka-band in the EEA, and therefore no overlap between the
         Parties’ activities arises in this narrow segment in the EEA.
(365)    Second, post-Transaction, the Parties will continue to face competition from a
         number of established GEO and new LEO players, both at the global and EEA-
         wide level.
(366)    The results of the market investigation indicated that several competitors will
         remain post-Transaction. In particular, the majority of respondents having
         expressed a view, submitted that there are sufficient alternative suppliers to the
         Parties in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, including in any
         potential segments thereof, globally and in the EEA. (431) Respondents consider
         that such suppliers include Arabsat/Hellas Sat, Avanti Communications,
         Echostar, Eutelsat, Hispasat, SpaceCom, Starlink, Telesat, Turksat, Space
         Norway and Viasat/Inmarsat. (432)

                                                 101
(367)    Further, the market investigation also confirmed that LEOs compete or at least
         pose a competitive constraint in the market for the supply of two-way satellite
         capacity. In this respect, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view
         submitted that LEO operators (e.g., Starlink or Eutelsat’s OneWeb) are currently
         a credible alternative in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity,
         including in any potential segments thereof. (433)
(368)    In addition, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view expect new
         entry or expansion in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity in
         the next 5 years, primarily by LEO operators (such as Amazon’s Project Kuiper
         system). (434) For instance, a respondent noted that ‘over the next five years, new
         competitors are expected to enter or expand in the market for the supply of two-
         way satellite capacity, intensifying competition across various segments. Notably,
         Amazon’s Project Kuiper is positioned to become a significant player, with plans
         to deploy a large LEO constellation to provide services globally. Additionally,
         several Chinese companies are advancing their satellite initiatives, with the
         SpaceSail project standing out […]. Other notable projects include Telesat’s
         Lightspeed LEO constellation and OneWeb’s ongoing expansion efforts to
         enhance its global LEO network’. Another respondent stated that ‘we expect to
         see several more players enter the market. These include, but are not limited to:
         Amazon Kuiper; Telesat LightSpeed; Meridian Space […]. Also, China has
         announced intent to enter at LEO’. (435)
(369)    Therefore, post-Transaction the Parties will continue to be constrained by a
         sufficient number of alternative GEO, as well as LEO, suppliers of two-way
         satellite capacity.
(370)    Third, the market investigation confirmed that there is spare capacity available in
         the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity market, which may be
         further increased as a result of the planned entry or expansion by LEO
         constellations.
(371)    The majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that there exists
         sufficient spare capacity amongst the rivals of SES and Intelsat to cover
         customers’ demand for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, including in any
         potential segments thereof. (436) In this respect, one respondent stated that ‘[t]he
         launch of new High-Throughput Satellite (HTS) systems and Low Earth Orbit
         (LEO) constellations has significantly increased the supply of capacity, outpacing
         demand growth’. Another respondent noted that ‘taking into account the
         constellation of Starlink, and also OneWeb, there is a significant amount of
         capacity in orbit, even without considering the arrival of Kuiper’. A further
         responded submitted that ‘[t]he supply is significantly higher than the demand’
         (437) These responses imply that spare capacity could be used to meet demand if

                                                    102
         the merged entity were to increase prices for the supply of two-way capacity,
         including in its potential segments.
(372)    Fourth, the market investigation indicated that switching between suppliers of
         two-way satellite capacity may not be easy and quick, but is a realistic option for
         customers.
(373)    In particular, the majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that,
         although switching from one supplier of two-way satellite capacity to another is
         technically complex and needs some time, it still remains a real option. (438)
(374)    In this respect, respondents explained that the feasibility, cost, and time required
         for switching ‘depends on several factors, such as the number of sites deployed
         and the ground segment technology in use’. Respondents also acknowledged that
         ‘[i]t requires time and extra capex to move the service to another satellite’ given
         that ‘the equipments and terminals need to be changed, as well as antennas […]
         need to be repositioned’ – for instance ‘if a customer has a large number of
         remote sites connected, migrating to a new supplier could be costly and time-
         consuming due to the need for site visits to reconfigure or replace equipment at
         each location’. (439)
(375)    However, at the same time, respondents noted that, although switching from one
         supplier to another can be costly and complex (with varying degrees of difficulty),
         ‘it is certainly feasible and has been done on many occasions in the past’.
         Respondents also added that switching would make sense ‘for a disruptive
         technology/performances and a TCO (ie; terminal cost and monthly subscription
         fees) providing economies in the mid-long term’, when customers ‘identify new
         solutions offering better pricing or performance’, or for reasons related to
         ‘reliability’ and ‘changes in pricing and general terms and conditions’. (440)
(376)    Fifth, the results of the market investigation indicate that customers hold a degree
         of bargaining power vis-a-vis suppliers of two-way satellite capacity.
(377)    In particular, the majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         customers have medium to strong negotiation power when purchasing of two-way
         satellite capacity. (441) For example, a respondent submitted that ‘customers can
         obtain favorable terms when purchasing two-way satellite capacities. Typically,
         customers initiate competitive purchasing processes, inviting multiple bidders,
         which enhances their negotiating and bargaining power. Additionally, satellite
         capacity prices have been continuously decreasing in recent years due to an
         oversupply relative to demand and the introduction of new satellite systems that
         enable lower pricing’. Another respondent stated that ‘[b]ecause of emerging
         competition, customers' ability to obtain favourable terms for two-way satellite
         capacity is between medium and strong’. In the same vein, a further respondent
         noted that ‘[d]ue to the entry of LEO megaconstellations (e.g., Starlink), currently
         overall two-way satellite capacity supply exceeds demand, hence exerting
         pressure on capacity price and giving more bargaining power to the customers.
         In GEO in particular, we observe a shift of the demand from GEO to NGSO
         (cannibalization) leading to pressure on capacity prices’. (442)

                                                 103
(378)    Furthermore, the majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         when negotiating the extension of an existing agreement for the supply of two-
         way satellite capacity, customers are not only able to extend the initially agreed
         terms and conditions, but are also in a position to re-negotiate each of the terms
         and conditions of such an agreement with their supplier. (443)
(379)    Finally, the market investigation confirmed that customers multi-source their
         requirements for two-way satellite capacity. (444) Customers explained that they
         multi-source to achieve global coverage, optimize performance and meet
         operational needs. They also explained that ‘maintaining relationships with
         several suppliers improves bargaining power, enabling customers to negotiate
         more favorable terms when purchasing satellite capacity’, as well as that ‘[f]rom
         a negotiation standpoint, we can get better pricing by competing business with
         multiple operators with similar coverages and capacity’. (445) These responses
         imply that working with more than one provider can provide customers with
         additional leverage when negotiating contracts for the supply of two-way satellite
         capacity.
(380)    Sixth, based on the feedback received from market participants during the market
         investigation and evidence included in third-party industry reports, but also as
         previously recognised by the Commission in Viasat/Inmarsat (446), satellite
         operators appear to be increasingly shifting away from traditional wide-beam
         satellites towards HTS.
(381)    HTS satellites cost less and have a higher volume capacity and a lower latency
         compared to traditional wide-beam satellites. These advantages may make them
         more suitable and attractive to certain customers, in particular in data intensive
         industries.
(382)    Although the results of the market investigation were inconclusive on the question
         whether wide-beam and HTS two-way capacity constitute a single or separate
         markets, more respondents having expressed a view submitted that these are
         substitutable from a demand-side perspective. Notably, even some respondents
         who replied that wide-beam and HTS should not be viewed as substitutable,
         acknowledged that the market is transitioning towards HTS.
(383)    In this respect – as mentioned above at paragraph 116 – a respondent to the market
         investigation submitted that ‘it is ‘easier to switch from wide beam to HTS than
         from HTS to wide beam’. Another respondent noted that ‘substituting HTS for
         wide-beam capacity for two-way services is reasonable and potentially favorable
         as well. Conversely, substituting wide-beam services for HTS services is unlikely
         to meet the capacity requirements for a given coverage area’. The same
         respondent also added that ‘[w]ide-beam services are predominately used for
         broadcast services. For two-way services, most MNOs have adopted HTS
         servicesfor their customer service requirements, but it is not uncommon for SNOs
         to have single satellites that provide both wide-beam and HTS coverages’. A
         further respondent submitted that there exists ‘[o]ne-sided interchangeability –
         transition from wide beam to HTS’. Another respondent noted that ‘[m]arket is
         migrating from wide-beam to HTS’. (447)

    )   Replies to Q2 (Two-way capacity), questions C.A.7.1 and C.A.8.1.
