(384)    These responses imply that even if the market transition from wide-beam to HTS
         satellites is still in progress, the increased demand for and use of two-way HTS
         capacity can pose a competitive constraint on SNOs operating traditional wide-
         beam GEO satellites.
(385)    Furthermore, industry forecasts estimate that NGSO HTS satellite capacity will
         account for the vast majority of market value growth between 2019 and 2033.
         This is shown in Figure 3 below, based on data from the Novaspace industry
         report.
                      Figure 3: Depiction of video distribution via different technologies




                     Source: Novaspace, ‘Satellite Connectivity and Video Market’, September 2024, page 61

(386)    Novaspace estimates that HTS revenues will grow an additional USD 17.3 billion
         between 2023 and 2033, of which only 4% of the growth will stem from GEO,
         and 96% from NGSO. On the other hand, GEO wide-beam capacity revenue is
         expected to decrease by USD 3.3 billion over the same period, falling to USD 2.6
         billion. NGSO HTS is expected to represent 72% of overall market value by
         2033. (448)
(387)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that due to the ongoing market
         transition from wide-beam to HTS systems, and the expectation that this
         movement will continue in the future, the merged entity will be competitively
         constrained by HTS alternatives in the narrowly defined markets comprising
         solely wide-beam two-way satellite capacity.
(388)    Seventh, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         the Transaction will have a neutral impact on their company and on competition
         in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, including in any
         potential segments thereof. (449)

6.4.2.3. Conclusion
(389)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
         not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
         of horizontal effects in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity and
         any potential segments thereof.




(448)   Form CO, para. 426.
(449)   Replies to Q2 (Two-way capacity), questions E.1 and E.2.
                                                    105
6.5.      Horizontal assessment: Potential overlap in the market for the supply of
          satellite services in the commercial aviation industry
(390)     As mentioned above at paragraph (148), Intelsat is active in the supply of satellite
          services in the commercial aviation industry. SES is not currently active on this
          market. However, SES may in the future supply satellite services directly to
          commercial aviation customers via Airbus’ HBCplus programme.
(391)     The HBCplus programme gives airlines the flexibility to choose their preferred
          service provider for IFC within an Airbus catalogue. SES, among others (such as
          Viasat/Inmarsat) is one of the service providers that may be selected. Under the
          HBCplus programme, the customer terminal is installed by Airbus for use with
          the selected SSP. SES and Airbus entered into a Letter of Agreement with respect
          of the HBCplus programme in April 2022, and a definitive agreement with respect
          of same was finalized in early 2023. SES is currently working on a number of
          opportunities in relation to the HBCplus programme, some of which are indirect
          (in which SES participates as a supplier of satellite capacity), and some of which
          are direct (in which SES will be providing IFC directly). […]. (450)
(392)     In view of the above, a potential overlap between the Parties’ activities arises in
          the market for the supply of satellite services in the commercial aviation industry,
          in which Intelsat has an established presence and SES is a potential entrant.

6.5.1.    Notifying Party’s views
(393)     The Notifying Party considers that the Transaction will not give rise to
          competition concerns in the market for the supply of satellite services in the
          commercial aviation industry, or any of its potential segments, for the following
          reasons.
(394)     First, Intelsat’s market share in the market for the supply of satellite services in
          the commercial aviation industry is moderate (up to [20-30]% at the global level
          and up to [10-20]% in the EEA). (451)
(395)     Second, the market for the supply of satellite services in the commercial aviation
          industry is highly competitive and contains multiple alternative competitors that
          would compete with the merged entity post-Transaction. In particular, a number
          of GEO satellite operators, as well as new entrant LEO satellite operators would
          continue to rigorously compete with the merged entity to equip and serve
          commercial aircrafts. Competing GEO providers of satellite services to the
          commercial aviation industry include Viasat/Inmarsat, Panasonic Avionics,
          Anuvu, Thales, and others. LEO competitors include Echostar Hughes and
          Starlink, which is rapidly winning meaningful market share. (452)

6.5.2.    Commission’s assessment
(396)     The Commission considers that, for the reasons set out below, the Transaction
          does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a
          result of potential horizontal effects in the market for the supply of satellite
          services in the commercial aviation industry or any of its potential segments, at
          worldwide or EEA-wide level.



(450)    Form CO, paras 1113-16.
(451)    Form CO, para. 1206.
(452)    Form CO, paras 1117-21.
                                              106
(397)    First, Intelsat has a modest share in the market for the supply of satellite services
         in the commercial aviation industry (below 30% at the global level, and below
         20% in the EEA).
(398)    Second, a wide range of competitors will remain post-Transaction.
(399)    In the market investigation, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a
         view consider that there are sufficient alternative suppliers to Intelsat in the
         market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation industry, and any
         potential segments thereof, at the global and EEA-wide level. (453) Such
         alternative suppliers would include Anuvu, Hughes, Panasonic Avionics,
         Starlink, Thales and Viasat/Inmarsat. (454)
(400)    In addition, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view consider
         that satellite services provided by LEO suppliers are currently a credible
         alternative in the market for the supply of satellite services for the aviation
         industry, and any potential segments thereof. (455) For instance, one respondent
         stated that ‘LEO satellite suppliers like Starlink or Eutelsat’s OneWeb already
         have customers in the aviation space’. Another respondent noted that ‘[…]
         satellite services provided by LEO suppliers are becoming credible alternatives
         in the market. While their market share is still emerging, their technological
         advantages such as lower latency, higher throughput, and global coverage
         position them as increasingly competitive options. Although LEO networks are
         still being fully deployed, these providers are already in active negotiations and
         have won contracts in business aviation. LEO suppliers are not yet predominant
         in global aviation connectivity, which remains led by traditional GEO but more
         and more they are gaining a market position and being considered for future
         aircraft connectivity programs’. (456)
(401)    Third, the majority of customers having expressed a view submitted that they
         would not consider switching to SES’s upcoming offering through the Airbus
         HBCplus programme, in the next 3-5 years. (457) This indicates that SES’s
         upcoming offering is not expected to become a particularly credible or disruptive
         service that would exert a significant constraining influence on the market for the
         supply of satellite services in the commercial aviation industry, in a short to
         medium timeframe.
(402)    Fourth, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         the Transaction will have a neutral or positive impact on their company and on
         competition on the market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation
         industry, and any potential segments thereof. (458)

6.5.3.    Conclusion
(403)     In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
          not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
          of potential horizontal effects in the market for the supply of satellite services in
          the commercial aviation industry and any potential segments thereof.

6.6.      Non-horizontal assessment
(404)     Based on the market shares provided in Section 6.3, the Notifying Party has
          identified four vertical relationships that give rise to affected markets: (459) (460)
          (a)     the supply of two-way satellite capacity (upstream) and the supply of
                  satellite services in the commercial aviation industry (downstream);
          (b)     the supply of two-way satellite capacity (upstream) and the supply of
                  satellite services in the maritime industry (downstream);
          (c)     the supply of two-way satellite capacity (upstream) and the supply of
                  satellite services in the fixed data industry (downstream); and
          (d)     the supply of two-way satellite capacity (upstream) and the supply of
                  satellite services in the government sector (downstream).

          markets.

(406)     As is shown in Table 52 above, in each case vertically affected markets arise
          because the upstream combined market share of the Parties exceeds 30%. The
          individual or combined market share of SES and Intelsat does not exceed 30% in
          any of the relevant downstream markets.
(407)     With respect to the risk of customer foreclosure, the Commission observes that
          with a presence of below 30% in the downstream markets for the supply of
          satellite services in each of the aviation, maritime, fixed data and government
          industries, the merged entity would not enjoy a significant degree of market power
          downstream and, therefore, would not have the ability to engage in customer
          foreclosure post-Transaction.
(408)     This is further supported by the results of the market investigation, which
          confirmed that several competing SSPs will remain post-Transaction. In
          particular, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view indicated that
          there are sufficient alternative suppliers to the Parties in the downstream markets
          for the supply of satellite services in the aviation, maritime, fixed data and
          government industries, and any potential segments thereof, at the global and EEA-
          wide level. In relation to the market for the supply of satellite service in the fixed
          data industry, the vast majority of respondents has equally confirmed that that
          sufficient alternative suppliers also exist at regional level. (461) In addition, the
          results of the market investigation confirmed that satellite services provided by
          LEO operators compete, or at least exert a competitive constraint, on the
          downstream markets. In this respect, the vast majority of respondents having
          expressed a view indicated that satellite services provided by LEO suppliers (e.g.,
          Starlink or Eutelsat’s OneWeb) are currently a credible alternative in the market
          for the supply of satellite services for the aviation, maritime, fixed data and
          government industries, and any potential segments thereof. (462) Furthermore, the
          majority of respondents having expressed a view consider that the Transaction
          will have a neutral or positive impact on their company and on competition in the
          markets for the supply of satellite services in the aviation, maritime, fixed data
          and government industries, and any potential segments thereof. (463)
(409)     Therefore, in the remainder of this Decision the Commission assesses potential
          vertical concerns of input foreclosure, namely whether the merged entity could
          remove, restrict or degrade the quality, or increase the price, of its two-way
          satellite capacity (input) to downstream suppliers of satellite services in the
          commercial aviation, maritime, fixed data and government industries.

6.6.1.    Input foreclosure: Vertically affected market for the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the commercial aviation
          industry (downstream)

6.6.1.1. Notifying Party’s views
(410)     The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will not result in any
          competition concerns arising from the vertical relationship between the upstream
          market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, and the downstream market



(461)    Replies to Q3 (Aviation), Q4 (Maritime), Q5 (Government), and Q6 (Fixed data), question D.1.
(462)    Replies to Q3 (Aviation), question D.9; Replies to Q4 (Maritime), question D.9; Replies to Q5
         (Government), question D.11; Replies to Q6 (Fixed data), question D.11.
(463)    Replies to Q3 (Aviation), Q4 (Maritime), Q5 (Government), and Q6 (Fixed data), questions E.1 and
         E.2.
                                                   110
         for the supply of satellite services in the commercial aviation industry, for the
         following reasons.
(411)    First, the Parties would have no ability to engage in input foreclosure. The merged
         entity would not have significant market power upstream, given there are many
         rival suppliers of two-way satellite capacity, and there are limited barriers to
         switching. (464) As satellite capacity is fungible across industry segments, in
         practice any SNO would be capable of supplying satellite capacity to SSPs for the
         provision of IFC in the commercial aviation industry and would, therefore,
         constrain the Parties post-Transaction. These include traditional SNOs such as
         Eutelsat, Telesat, Hispasat, APT Satellite / China SatCom, Space Norway and
         JSat-Sky Perfect, as well as Viasat/Inmarsat and Starlink. Due to this intense
         competition upstream, the Parties face downward pricing pressure from their SSP
         customers. (465)
(412)    Second, even if there were an ability to foreclose inputs, any change in the
         incentive to engage in input foreclosure would be minimal because there is no
         increment arising from the Transaction in relation to the supply of satellite
         services in the commercial aviation industry, given that SES is not currently active
         in this downstream market. In addition, Intelsat’s downstream share of supply is
         modest, and it faces strong competition from a number of competitors, including
         Viasat/Inmarsat, Panasonic Avionics, Thales and Anuvu, as well as from LEO
         operators, such as Starlink and Eutelsat’s OneWeb. (466)
(413)    Even if the Parties were to attempt to worsen the terms of supply of satellite
         capacity to SSPs for commercial aviation end uses, they would face two sources
         of constraint: (i) First, the Parties’ SSP customers could acquire satellite capacity
         from the Parties’ competitors. There is no shortage of satellite capacity to supply
         SSPs active in the aviation industry, and there is sufficient spare capacity in the
         industry; and (ii) Second, an attempted worsening of terms would make it more
         likely that SSPs would lose business to vertically integrated competitors, such as
         Viasat/Inmarsat and Starlink, which would in turn reduce the Parties’ revenues
         from supplying satellite capacity to SSPs. (467)
(414)    Given these constraints, if the Parties were to attempt an input foreclosure
         strategy, they would bear the full cost of such a strategy and would not reap the
         benefits as their SSP customers would switch to alternative satellite capacity
         suppliers and/or those SSP’ commercial airline customers would switch to using
         vertically integrated suppliers, such as Viasat/Inmarsat or Starlink. (468)
(415)    Third, as the Parties would have no ability and no incentive to engage in input
         foreclosure, there could be no adverse effects on competition. (469)

6.6.1.2. Commission’s assessment
(416)    For the reasons set out below and based on the results of the market investigation,
         the Commission considers that the merged entity would not have the ability to
         foreclose the access of downstream competitors in the aviation industry to its two-
         way satellite capacity. Therefore, given that the conditions under the Non-


(464)   Form CO, paras 1154, 1205, 1254, 1306, 1355, 1391, 1436, 1516.
(465)   Form CO, paras 1090-93.
(466)   Form CO, paras 1206-08, 1255, 1307, 1356, 1392, 1437, 1517.
(467)   Form CO, para. 1095.
(468)   Form CO, para. 1096.
(469)   Form CO, paras 1096, 1209, 1256, 1308, 1357, 1393, 1438, 1518.
                                                111
         Horizontal Merger Guidelines are cumulative (470), the Commission does not need
         to take a position of whether the two other conditions are satisfied.

6.6.1.2.1.      Ability to foreclose
(417)    For the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the merged entity
         would not have the ability to engage in a strategy of foreclosing downstream
         providers of satellite services in the aviation industry from accessing its two-way
         satellite capacity.
(418)    First, for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission considers that the merged
         entity does not have a significant degree of market power in the upstream market
         for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, including in any potential segments
         thereof, at the global or EEA-wide level.
(419)    The guidance set out in the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines sets out that ‘for
         input foreclosure to be a concern, the vertically integrated firm resulting from the
         merger must have a significant degree of market power in the upstream market.
         It is only in these circumstances that the merged firm can be expected to have a
         significant influence on the conditions of competition in the upstream market and
         thus, possibly, on prices and supply conditions in the downstream market’. (471)
(420)    As a result of the Commission’s conclusions regarding the assessment of
         horizontal effects in the market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity (see
         Section 6.4.2.2 above), the Commission considers that post-Transaction the
         Parties would not enjoy a significant degree of market power in the market for the
         supply of two-way satellite capacity, including in any potential segments thereof.
         In this respect reference is being made to the results of the market investigation
         and the Commission’s findings as detailed in Section 6.4.2.2 above. In summary:
         (1) the merged entity’s combined market position in the supply of two-way
         satellite capacity would be generally moderate; (2) post-Transaction the Parties
         will continue to face competition from several established GEO and new LEO
         players; (3) there exists sufficient spare two-way satellite capacity; (4) switching
         between suppliers of two-way satellite capacity may be technically complex and
         need some time, but remains a real option; (5) SPPs customers of two-way
         satellite capacity are able to exert bargaining power; (6) in the narrow defined
         segments comprising solely wide-beam two-way satellite capacity, the merged
         entity will be competitively constrained by HTS alternatives; and (7) the overall
         impact of the Transaction on the upstream market for the supply of two-way
         satellite capacity is expected to be neutral.
(421)    In light of the above considerations, the Commission considers that the merged
         entity does not have a significant degree of market power in the upstream market
         for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, and therefore would not have the
         ability to foreclose downstream rivals.
(422)    Second, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         vertical integration in the market for the supply of satellite services for the
         aviation industry gives the vertically integrated company a competitive advantage
         over its non-vertically integrated competitors. (472)



(470)   Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paras 93-118.
(471)   Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paras 23 and 35.
(472)   Replies to Q3 (Aviation), question D.5.
                                                 112
(423)    However, the market investigation yielded mixed feedback on whether it could
         be possible the merged entity to restrict access of downstream competitors to its
         two-way satellite capacity. A number of respondents having expressed a view
         submitted that it would be unlikely or not possible for the merged entity to
         remove, restrict or degrade the quality of the supply, or increase the price, of its
         satellite capacity to SSPs in the market for the supply of satellite services in the
         aviation industry, whereas an equal number of respondents indicated that such a
         strategy could be likely or very likely. (473)
(424)    Nonetheless, half of the respondents indicating that an input foreclosure strategy
         could be likely or very likely, either did not substantiate their response or
         submitted that there exist a number of market factors that would actually constrain
         the merged entity from engaging in input foreclosure. For instance, a customer
         noted that a foreclosure strategy ‘is possible but not probable given the
         proliferation of LEO providers and the influence this is having on pricing’. A
         competitor stated that ‘several practical and strategic factors could
         counterbalance this [foreclosure] risk: (1) Contractual Obligations: The merged
         entity is bound by existing contracts with independent SSPs, which are difficult to
         breach without incurring significant penalties. These agreements ensure
         continued supply to SSPs in the short term (while the contracts are in place),
         limiting the ability to restrict access or raise prices immediately. (2) Maximizing
         Fleet Utilization: The merged entity’s extensive satellite fleet requires a strategy
         focused on maximizing fill-rates to achieve returns within the satellites’ limited
         lifetimes. Channelling all capacity through its own SSP is unlikely to be feasible
         given the fleet’s scale. Maintaining multiple market channels, including sales to
         independent SSPs, remains a more effective approach to ensure high utilization
         and revenue. (3) Competitive Market Dynamics: The satellite services market
         faces intense competition from Low Earth Orbit (LEO) operators like Starlink,
         which are capturing market share […]. Restricting capacity to independent SSPs
         could alienate key partners, pushing them toward LEO providers or other GEO
         competitors. Maintaining strong relationships with SSPs is strategically
         important for the merged entity to counter these competitive pressures’. (474)
(425)    Third, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         the Transaction will have a neutral or positive impact on their company and on
         competition in the market for the supply of satellite services in the aviation
         industry, and any potential segments thereof. (475)

6.6.1.2.2.      Conclusion
(426)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
         not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
         of potential input foreclosure stemming from the vertical relationship between the
         Parties’ activities in the markets for the supply of two-way satellite capacity



(473)   Replies to Q3 (Aviation), question D.6. Furthermore, the feedback from the market investigation,
        in line with the arguments of the Notifying Party, indicated that there is no plausible vertical link
        between the supply of two-way satellite capacity via the C-band (upstream) and the supply of
        satellite services in the commercial aviation industry (downstream). This is due to the size of
        antennas for C-band satellite capacity which tend to have a large diameter making them impractical
        for aviation end uses (Form CO, paras 1074-75; Replies to Q2 (Two-way capacity), question
        C.A.9.1.

                                                    113
          (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the commercial aviation industry
          (downstream).

6.6.2.    Input foreclosure: Vertically affected market for the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry
          (downstream)

6.6.2.1. Notifying Party’s views
(427)     The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will not result in any input
          foreclosure concerns arising from the vertical relationship between the upstream
          market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, and the downstream market
          for the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry, for the following
          reasons.
(428)     First, the Parties would have no ability to engage in input foreclosure. The merged
          entity would not have significant market power upstream, given there are many
          rival suppliers of two-way satellite capacity, and there are limited barriers to
          switching. (476)
(429)     Second, even if there were an ability to foreclose the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity to downstream competitors, any change in the incentive to engage in
          input foreclosure would be minimal because there is no increment arising from
          the Transaction in relation to the supply of satellite services in the maritime
          industry, given that Intelsat is not active in this downstream market. (477)
(430)     In addition, SES’s individual downstream market share is de minimis ([0-5]% at
          the global level, and below [5-10]% in the EEA) and it faces strong competition
          from a number of competitors, including Marlink, Viasat/Inmarsat, KVH, Ditel,
          Navarino, and Speedcast. (478)
(431)     SES also faces significant competition from new LEO entrants. For instance,
          Starlink and OneWeb represent a significant competitive constraint in the
          maritime industry. (479)
(432)     Third, as the Parties would have no ability and no incentive to engage in input
          foreclosure, there could be no adverse effects on competition. (480)

6.6.2.2. Commission’s assessment
(433)     For the reasons set out below and based on the results of the market investigation,
          the Commission considers that the merged entity would not have the ability to
          foreclose the access of downstream competitors in the maritime industry to its
          two-way satellite capacity. Therefore, given that the conditions under the Non-
          Horizontal Merger Guidelines are cumulative (481), the Commission does not need
          to take a position of whether the two other conditions are satisfied.

6.6.2.2.1.       Ability to foreclose
(434)     For the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the merged entity
          would not have the ability to engage in a strategy of foreclosing downstream

(476)    Form CO, paras 1155, 1162, 1225, 1273, 1324, 1448, 1486.
(477)    Form CO, paras 1163, 1226, 1274, 1325, 1449, 1487.

          providers of satellite services in the maritime industry from accessing its two-way
          satellite capacity.
(435)     First, for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission considers that the merged
          entity does not have a significant degree of market power in the market for the
          supply of two-way satellite capacity, at the global and EEA-wide level, for the
          reasons set out above in paragraphs (418)-(421).
(436)     Second, market participants that expressed a view considered that vertical
          integration in the market for the supply of satellite services for the maritime
          industry gives the vertically integrated company a competitive advantage over its
          non-vertically integrated competitors. (482) However, market participants
          submitted that it would not be possible for the merged entity to remove, restrict
          or degrade the quality of the supply, or increase the price, of its satellite capacity
          to SSPs in the market for the supply of satellite services for the maritime industry.
          This is because ‘[t]he growing dominance of Starlink means that providers are
          unlikely to have the ability to implement such strategies. SSPs can just add a
          Starlink or OneWeb terminal (and SSPs already resell Starlink capacity)’. (483)
(437)     Third, all market participants that expressed a view considered that the
          Transaction will have a neutral impact on their company and on competition in
          the market for the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry. (484)

6.6.2.2.2.       Conclusion
(438)     In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
          not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
          of potential input foreclosure stemming from the vertical relationship between the
          Parties’ activities in the markets for the supply of two-way satellite capacity
          (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the maritime industry
          (downstream).

6.6.3.    Input foreclosure: Vertically affected market for the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry
          (downstream)

6.6.3.1. Notifying Party’s views
(439)     The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will not result in any
          competition concerns arising from the vertical relationship between the upstream
          market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, and the downstream market
          for the supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry, for the following
          reasons.


(482)    Replies to Q4 (Maritime), question D.5. The feedback from a market participant that is not active
         in the maritime industry, but has nonetheless replied to Q4 (Maritime), has been disregarded. The
         instructions of Q4 (Maritime) specifically ask market participants to reply only to those questions
         in relation to which they have sufficient knowledge.
(483)    Replies to Q4 (Maritime), question D.6. The feedback from a market participant that is not active
         in the maritime industry but, has nonetheless replied to Q4 (Maritime), has been disregarded. The
         instructions of Q4 (Maritime) specifically ask market participants to reply only to those questions
         in relation to which they have sufficient knowledge.
(484)    Replies to Q4 (Maritime), questions E.1 and E.2. The feedback from a market participant that is not
         active in the maritime industry, but has nonetheless replied to Q4 (Maritime), has been disregarded.
         The instructions of Q4 (Maritime) specifically ask market participants to reply only to those
         questions in relation to which they have sufficient knowledge.
                                                    115
(440)    First, the Parties would have no ability to engage in input foreclosure. The merged
         entity would not have significant market power upstream, given that there are
         many rival suppliers of two-way satellite capacity, and there are limited barriers
         to switching. (485)
(441)    Second, even if there were an ability to foreclose the supply of two-way satellite
         capacity to downstream competitors, the Parties would have no incentive to
         attempt a foreclosure strategy. The Parties’ combined shares downstream are de
         minimis. (486) This means that the Parties would bear the full cost of a hypothetical
         input foreclosure strategy (in that it would lose sales of satellite capacity to
         downstream competitors) but would reap only a small fraction of any hypothetical
         benefits with such a low combined share downstream. (487)
(442)    In addition, as fibre networks and 5G have expanded recently, many fixed data
         customers have transitioned away from using satellites, decreasing revenue
         opportunities for satellite operators. As fibre continues to expand, customers in
         the fixed data industry will no longer need satellite services or will be able to
         reduce their reliance on it to simply serve as a back-up connection option.
(443)    In any event, competition within the fixed data industry remains fierce. SES and
         Intelsat face, and will continue to face, competition from a range of legacy GEO
         satellite providers, including Echostar/Hughes, Viasat/Inmarsat, Hispasat, Telesat
         and Eutelsat. Moreover, customers increasingly make use of the low-cost, low-
         latency, high-bandwidth solutions offered by LEO operators, such as Starlink and
         others. As such, any hypothetical attempt by the Parties to foreclose competitors
         would leave the customers of such competitors with a range of alternative
         suppliers, other than the Parties, they could switch to. The Parties’ low combined
         shares downstream suggest that customers switching from a competitor that the
         Parties tried to foreclose would be more likely to switch to rivals, than to the
         Parties. (488)
(444)    Third, as the Parties would have no ability and no incentive to engage in input
         foreclosure, there could be no adverse effects on competition. (489)

6.6.3.2. Commission’s assessment
(445)    For the reasons set out below and based on the results of the market investigation,
         the Commission considers that the merged entity would not have the ability to
         foreclose the access of downstream competitors in the fixed data industry to its
         two-way satellite capacity. Therefore, given that the conditions under the Non-
         Horizontal Merger Guidelines are cumulative (490), the Commission does not need
         to take a position of whether the two other conditions are satisfied.

6.6.3.2.1.      Ability to foreclose
(446)    For the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the merged entity
         would not have the ability to engage in a strategy of foreclosing downstream

(487)   Form CO, paras 1172, 1232, 1280, 1331, 1368, 1411, 1456, 1493.
(488)   Form CO, paras 1173-75.
(489)   Form CO, paras 1176, 1233, 1281, 1332, 1369, 1412, 1457, 1494.
(490)   Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paras 93-118.
                                                 116
         providers of satellite services in the fixed data industry from accessing its two-
         way satellite capacity.
(447)    First, for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission considers that the merged
         entity does not have a significant degree of market power in the market for the
         supply of two-way satellite capacity, at the global or EEA-wide level, for the
         reasons set out above in paragraphs (418)-(421).
(448)    Second, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         vertical integration in the market for the supply of satellite services for the fixed
         data industry gives the vertically integrated company a competitive advantage
         over its non-vertically integrated competitors. (491)
(449)    However, the majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that it
         would be unlikely or not possible for the merged entity to remove, restrict or
         degrade the quality of the supply, or increase the price, of its satellite capacity to
         SSPs in the market for the supply of satellite services for the fixed data industry.
         (492) One respondent explained that this is because of ‘the presence of other
         satellite network operators which act as ready alternatives in the event where
         anti-competitive action is taken by another satellite network operator’. Another
         respondent stated that the market for the supply of satellite services in the fixed
         data industry is a ‘competitive market and the list of other providers is still big
         enough’. A further respondent explained that ‘the introduction of LEO
         competitors – notably, Starlink – has fundamentally changed customer
         expectations in terms of price and performance. Many of our customers are
         specifying LEO over GEO and GEO is only able to compete on a price basis since
         the performance of GEO beams cannot match that of LEO for our specific
         customer base. […] Additionally, more LEO constellations are anticipated to
         enter service in the coming years (including Amazon Kuiper), which will only
         create more options for customers’. Another respondent stated that ‘[there] are
         many alternatives and the supply in higher than the demand, the prices will not
         increase’. (493)
(450)    In addition, the majority of respondents that expressed a view indicated that in a
         hypothetical scenario, where the merged entity would engage in input foreclosure,
         it would be possible for them to source satellite capacity from alternative capacity
         providers and continue to viably compete on the market for the supply of satellite
         services in the fixed data industry. (494)
(451)    Furthermore, the market investigation indicated that in the fixed data industry
         fibre connectivity solutions pose a constraint on SSPs. In particular, the majority
         of respondents having expressed a view considered that connectivity services
         provided via fibre technologies are currently a credible alternative to the supply
         of connectivity services provided via satellite in the fixed data industry, and any
         potential segments thereof. (495) As such, the existence of credible fibre
         alternatives, at least in geographic areas with developed fibre networks, could
         make a hypothetical foreclosure strategy even less plausible, as customers of
         foreclosed SSPs could switch to fibre alternatives instead of switching to the
         merged entity.

(452)     Third, the vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
          the Transaction will have a neutral impact on their company and on competition
          in the market for the supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry, and any
          potential segments thereof. (496)

6.6.3.2.2.      Conclusion
(453)     In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
          not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
          of potential input foreclosure stemming from the vertical relationship between the
          Parties’ activities in the markets for the supply of two-way satellite capacity
          (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the fixed data industry
          (downstream).

6.6.4.    Input foreclosure: Vertically affected market for the supply of two-way satellite
          capacity (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the government sector
          (downstream)

6.6.4.1. Notifying Party’s views
(454)     The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will not result in any
          competition concerns arising from the vertical relationship between the upstream
          market for the supply of two-way satellite capacity, and the downstream market
          for the supply of satellite services in the government sector, for the following
          reasons.
(455)     First, the Parties would have no ability to engage in input foreclosure. The merged
          entity would not have a significant market power upstream, given that there are
          many rival suppliers of two-way satellite capacity, and there are limited barriers
          to switching. (497) As satellite capacity is fungible across industry segments, in
          practice any SNO would be capable of supplying satellite capacity to SSPs for the
          provision of satellite services in the government sector and would, therefore,
          constrain the Parties post-Transaction. These include SNOs such as
          Eutelsat/OneWeb, ABS Asia Broadcast Satellite, ArabSat/Hellas Sat, Hispasat
          and Telesat. (498)
(456)     The intense competition in the supply of satellite capacity, including to the
          government sector, would also be reflected in evidence showing that pricing of
          satellite capacity has been declining (from 1 496 USD/Mbps per month to 310
          USD/Mbps per month in the period 2019-24). (499) This would mean that, even if
          the merged entity were to attempt to worsen the terms of supply of satellite
          capacity to SSPs for government end uses, those SSPs could acquire satellite
          capacity from the Parties’ competitors. (500)
(457)     Furthermore, there is no shortage of satellite capacity to supply SSPs active in the
          government sector, and there is sufficient spare capacity in the industry. (501)




(496)    Replies to Q6 (Fixed data), questions E.1 and E.2.
(497)    Form CO, para. 1184.
(498)    Form CO, para. 1185.
(499)    Novaspace’s ‘Satellite Connectivity and Video Market’ report, 31st edition (2024), file
         ‘PREMIUM_SCVM24_FSS_capacity_pricing.xlsx’, tab ‘Consolidated ARPU_Mbps’.
(500)    Form CO, paras 1186-87.
(501)    Form CO, paras 1187, 1237-43, 1286-94, 1337-44, 1373-80, 1418-25, 1462-69, 1498-1505.
                                               118
(458)    Second, even if there were an ability to foreclose inputs, the merged entity would
         have no incentive to attempt a foreclosure strategy.
(459)    The Parties’ combined shares downstream are modest (just [10-20]% globally and
         [10-20]% in the EEA), and the increment is small ([0-5]% globally and [0-5]% in
         the EEA), which means that the change in incentives post-Transaction would be
         small. (502)
(460)    Moreover, if the Parties were to attempt an input foreclosure strategy, they would
         bear the full cost of such a strategy and would not reap the benefits. This is
         because their SSP customers could easily use satellite capacity from alternative
         suppliers (i.e., other than the Parties), and/or government sector customers would
         switch to using alternative downstream competitors such as Viasat/Inmarsat or
         Starlink. (503)
(461)    In addition, within the supply of satellite services in the government sector, the
         Parties are significantly competitively constrained by both GEO and LEO
         operators (e.g., Starlink, OneWeb already offer services directly to government
         customers, whereas Amazon’s Project Kuiper and Telesat’s Lightspeed are also
         expected to supply government customers). (504)
(462)    As such, government sector customers switching away from SSPs as a result of a
         hypothetical foreclosure attempt by the merged entity would be able to switch to
         a range of alternative suppliers, not only to the Parties. The Parties’ modest
         combined shares (both globally and in the EEA) would suggest that customers
         switching from a competitor that the Parties tried to foreclose would be more
         likely to switch to rivals than to the Parties. (505)
(463)    Third, as the Parties would have no ability and no incentive to engage in input
         foreclosure, there could be no adverse effects on competition. (506)

6.6.4.2. Commission’s assessment
(464)    For the reasons set out below and based on the results of the market investigation,
         the Commission considers that the merged entity would not have the ability to
         foreclose the access of downstream competitors in the government sector to its
         two-way satellite capacity. Therefore, given that the conditions under the Non-
         Horizontal Merger Guidelines are cumulative (507), the Commission does not need
         to take a position of whether the two other conditions are satisfied.

6.6.4.2.1.      Ability to foreclose
(465)    For the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the merged entity
         would not have the ability to engage in a strategy of foreclosing downstream
         providers of satellite services in the government sector from accessing its two-
         way satellite capacity.
(466)    First, for the purposes of this Decision, the Commission considers that the merged
         entity does not have a significant degree of market power in the market for the

(504)   Form CO, paras 1192-94
(505)   Form CO, paras 1195, 1244-49, 1295-1300, 1345-50, 1381-86, 1426-31, 1470-75, 1506-11.

(507)   Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paras 93-118.
                                                119
         supply of two-way satellite capacity, at the global and EEA-wide level, for the
         reasons set out above in paragraphs (418)-(421).
(467)    Second, the majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that
         vertical integration in the market for the supply of satellite services for the
         government sector gives the vertically integrated company a competitive
         advantage over its non-vertically integrated competitors. (508) Furthermore, the
         vast majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that it would be
         likely or very likely for the merged entity to remove, restrict or degrade the quality
         of the supply, or increase the price, of its satellite capacity to SSPs in the market
         for the supply of satellite services for the government sector. (509) The
         Commission notes though that there was a very high number of “I don’t know”
         responses to this question.
(468)    Nonetheless, a large part of the respondents indicating that an input foreclosure
         strategy could be likely or very likely did not substantiate their response or
         submitted that there exist a number of market factors that would actually constrain
         the merged entity from engaging in input foreclosure. For instance, one market
         participant stated that ‘several practical and strategic factors could
         counterbalance this risk: (1) Contractual Obligations: The merged entity is bound
         by existing contracts with independent SSPs, which are difficult to breach without
         incurring significant penalties. These agreements ensure continued supply to
         SSPs in the short term (while the contracts are in place), limiting the ability to
         restrict access or raise prices immediately. (2) Maximizing Fleet Utilization: The
         merged entity’s extensive satellite fleet requires a strategy focused on maximizing
         fill-rates to achieve returns within the satellites’ limited lifetimes. Channelling all
         capacity through its own SSP is unlikely to be feasible given the fleet’s scale.
         Maintaining multiple market channels, including sales to independent SSPs,
         remains a more effective approach to ensure high utilization and revenue. (3)
         Competitive Market Dynamics: The satellite services market faces intense
         competition from Low Earth Orbit (LEO) operators like Starlink, which are
         capturing market share […]. Restricting capacity to independent SSPs could
         alienate key partners, pushing them toward LEO providers or other GEO
         competitors. Maintaining strong relationships with SSPs is strategically
         important for the merged entity to counter these competitive pressures’. Another
         market participant noted that ‘certain market and regulatory mechanisms (e.g.,
         EU/NATO institutional programs, open public tenders, and the competition
         framework) could limit the actual impact of such behavior, especially in the
         medium term’. (510)
(469)    In addition, despite several respondents expecting a negative impact of a potential
         input foreclosure strategy on their company (511), many respondents having
         expressed a view consider that it would be possible for them to source satellite
         capacity from alternative providers and continue to viably compete on the market
         for the supply of satellite services to the government sector. (512) In this regard, a
         strong majority of market participants consider that satellite services based on
         LEO satellites are a credible alternative in the market for the supply of satellite
         services in the government sector. (513) Of the few respondents that did not

         consider them to become a credible alternative within the next 5 years. (514)
(470)    Third, the majority of respondents having expressed a view considered that the
         Transaction will have a neutral or positive impact on their company and on
         competition in the market for the supply of satellite services in the government
         sector. (515)

6.6.4.2.2.      Conclusion
(471)    In light of the foregoing, the Commission concludes that the Transaction would
         not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result
         of potential input foreclosure stemming from the vertical relationship between the
         Parties’ activities in the markets for the supply of two-way satellite capacity
         (upstream) and the supply of satellite services in the government sector
         (downstream).


7.       CONCLUSION
(472)    For the above reasons, the European Commission has decided not to oppose the
         notified operation and to declare it compatible with the internal market and with
         the EEA Agreement. This decision is adopted in application of Article 6(1)(b) of
         the Merger Regulation and Article 57 of the EEA Agreement.
